Bank performance

The resutts today reflect the magnitude of the issues that have come te light since

| joined The Co-operative Bank ten months ago. In addition, as we outlined on the
24 March 2014, further costs have materialised since the completion of the Liability
Management Exercise (LME) in December 2013 as a result of a continuing review
of The Co-operative Bank’s legacy operations, assets and liabilities by the new
Executive Team.

During 2013 the task for the new management was to keep the Bank alive. The
successful completion of the LIME prevented the Bank from going into resolution,
preserving the Bank for our customers and protecting jobs without cost to the
taxpayer. However, there continue to be significant issues which need to be resolved.
As already announced on 24 March 2014, the management team’s continuing review
has unearthed a range of conduct and legal issues since December 2013 which has
contributed to the need to further strengthen the Bank’s capital position over and above
The Co-operative Group's 2014 contribution.

Whilst these risks were identified in the LME prospectus, the financial impact of these
additional items, together with the higher than expected cost of separation from The
Co-operative Group, means that the starting capital position of the Bank for the 4-5
year recovery period is weaker than in the plan announced last year. The proposed
capital raising will enable us to reset this starting point and continue with the execution
of our original business plan which remains unchanged.

It is early days but initial progress on our business plan is encouraging and we remain
enthusiastic about the long term potential for the Bank. We have started to simplify the
business; we are reducing costs and are currently ahead of schedule in de-risking our
assets. We are also beginning to fix the fundamentals of the Bank as we drive the change
needed to return to our roots as a bank focused on our retail and SME customers.

Community banking is the heart of our business and despite our recent troubles the
Core Business has seen significant stability. Year on year, our Core Retail deposit
balances are broadly unchanged. In the future, we will be investing in IT to both deliver
digital banking in a way that meets our customers’ expectations and also improve the
systems that lie behind our products. We will atso be asking our customers’ opinions
as we renew the values and ethical principles that remain at the centre of why they
choose to bank with us. There are still major hurdles ahead to overcome. The level

of change required in improvement in processes, systems and culture is significant.
We are determined to rebuild trust in the Bank after the events of last year and reward
the loyalty our customers and shareholders have shown us.

We appreciate that customers and other stakeholders continue to feel angry about
how past failings placed the future of the business so seriously at risk. | would like

to apologise to them, to thank them for their continued loyalty and to thank colleagues
for their commitment during such difficult times.

Niafl Booker
Chiet Executive

Our strategy

As part of the overall Recapitalisation Plan announced last year (see page 13 for

a fuller description of the plan), the Bank has taken a number of actions to address

the challenges it faces starting with the LME which was completed in December 2013.
In parallel with the raising of additional capital announced on 24 March 2014, the focus
now turns to implementing the Bank’s strategy of becoming, over the next four to five
years, a smaller and efficient bank retaining Co-operative values and focused on Retail
and SME customers.

Overarching strategy

The Bank’s overarching strategy, which mvolves a Turnaround Plan of
approximately four to five years, Is to leverage our historic brand strength
and high levels of customer service satisfaction to create, over time, an
efficent and profitable bank with a reduced overall risk profile and to
teduce our nsk weighted assets

This will require us to simplify and focus the Core business on Reiail
banking and smat! and medium sized business customers where we feel
we have strong existing market credentials, customer relatonships and
expertise, whilst achieving significant operational efficencies

Those assets which are not consistent with our strategy and classified
as part of the Non-core business will be actively managed to achieve the
most appropriate asset value on an individual portfolio basis or targeted
for run down or exit

Since joining the Bank ten months
ago, the challenges we face to turn the
business around have been the subject
of extensive comment. The results

we are announcing today reflect the
magnitude of these issues as well as
further costs which have materialised
since the completion of the Liability
Management Exercise (LIME).

We appreciate that our customers and stakeholders rightly contmue to feel
angry about how the Bank found itself in a financial situation which placed
the future of the busmess so seriously at nsk | would like to apologise to them
and thank our customers for the loyalty they have shown us over the last year
and our colleagues for their commitment and professionalism during such
difficult times

Performance review

The legacy issues the new management tearn and I have inherrted

will continue to impact the performance of the business for some time

The full year results for 2013 reflect the further impacts of previously reported
Issues of Imparment charges, predommantly in the Non-core business, the
wnte down on our IT assets as outlined at our mterims, mcreased conduct
nsk provisions (including PPi) and elevated operating costs Additionally, the
complexity and cost of separating the Bank from The Co-operative Group is
proving greater than originally expected and further conduct risk and legal
issues have emerged Whilst the Core Bank remains profitable at an operating
level, there ts no doubt that our financial difficulties and other tssues have
impacted the 2013 performance of the Core franchise

Stable liquidity and improved capital position

Against this financial picture, we have overcome some significant obstacles
Our focus on building liquidity enabled us to successfully navigate the
turbulence of the second half of the year and also take the steps requred
to improve our capital position The completion of the Liability Management
Exercise (LME) m December was a key achievement and added over

£1 2bn of CET1 before costs The scale of this achievement ts not to be
underestimated and we are immensely grateful for the support of our
subordinated bondholders

As announced on 24 March 2014 and detailed on page 22, we are m the
process of rarsing additional CET1 over and above the 2014 contribution from
The Co-operative Group upon which we reman dependent The complexity
of the Bank's separation from The Co-operative Group was outlined n the
LME prospectus and the higher than anticipated costs and tax implications
associated with it together with the additional conduct and legal risks have
Impacted on the Bank's capital position This additional capital will give us
further room to begin to implement our Tumaround Plan to reshape the Bank
around our Retail and SME customers

Simplifying the business

Creating a viable business that ts sustamabte in the long term means that

we must simplify our busmess and significantly reduce our costs We have
already taken a number of actions, announcing the closure of operations

in Guernsey and the Isle of Man and are in the early stages of discussions

to sell our stake in Unity Trust Bank. Alongside this, the cost reduction
programme has begun across the business and as part of this we have
previously announced a reduction in the branch network. These decisions are,
of course, difficult but are driven by the reality of the situation the Bank ts in

Chief Executive’s review continued

Focus on the Core Bank

Despite the unprecedented circumstances the Bank has taced, we

believe there are some real positives to build on as we focus our attention

on implementing that plan and creasing retums in the Core Bank. Customer
satisfaction has been historically strong, and Core Retail deposits have been
broadly stable reducing by less than one per cent in 2013 Our objective is to
build on our strength in the Retail business by deepenmg those relationships
whilst for our SME customers our plan is to deliver a straightforward banking
proposition focused on the communities in which the branches sit Work is
underway to develop this

Run down and exit in Non-core

A priority for the Bank 1s to reduce the level of Non-core assets, which are
inconsistent with the Bank's strategy and risk appetite, as we streamtine the
business and focus our efforts on supporting Retail and SME customers
These assets naw sit in the Non-core division called Co-operative Asset
Management (CoAM) and developing ext plans for these portfolios, which
balance maximum value, capital preservation, hquidity and the need to
strengthen our balance sheet, are well underway Qur target is to reduce
Non-core customer assets to c £14 Obn by the end of 2014 and Non-core
customer assets have already been reduced by a total of £2 1bn in 2013
through a combmation of formal trade sales, proactive rebankng of our
Corporate clients and the natural run off of the Corporate book We have
also successfully restructured a number of existing relationships

Preserving our Values and Ethics

Balancing the requirement to make these difficult changes to the busmess
with the need to preserve what makes us distinct is no easy task Itis critical
fo maintain our commitment to customer service and the Co-operative values:
that have set us apart for the last 140 years We recognise the importance of
ethics and values for all of our customers and remam committed to upholding
them This 1s why, for the first bme, the Bank's consttutan in the form of its
Articles of Association includes a commitment to act in line with the principles
and values associated with the Co-operative Movement and we have
established a Values and Ethics Committee to ensure that we adhere to them

We are seeking customers’ views as we review and renew our commitment
to values and ethics and wil be engaging customers in this process and
seeking them mput to our Ethical Policy tater in the Spring The Bank's Ethical
Policy has been renewed and expanded in line with customers’ views on four
separate occasions since rts launch m 1992 and it 1s only right that at ths
point in our history we engage with customers again The work to make this
happen has now begun and we will let customers know more mn the coming
months Further detats are found in the Values and Ethics Committee Report

Separation and transformation

From December last year the Bank’s ownership structure changed and &

4S no longer a wholly owned subsidiary of The Co-operative Group as 70%
of the Bank's shares are now owned by other shareholders The Bank s
therefore run and governed separately from The Co-operative Group with

Recapitalisation and the Liability
Management Exercise

Dunng the year the Bank cared out a refinancing exercise with the aim of
increasing the level of capital held This exercise mvolved issuing new shares
and new debt in exchange for cash and existing debt and preference shares

Background

The Bank received a letter from the Financial Services Authority (FSA), in
January 2013, addressed to the Board, notifying it of a revised Individual
Capital Guidance (CG) and greatly increased Capital Planning Buffer (CPB)
tobe held exclusively in Core Tier 1 capital Whilst the Bank had capital
suiticient to meet the revised ICG’, the letter acknowledged that the sze

of the CPB meant that the Bank would be i breach of this from the outset
The letter requested that the Board prepare, and present to the Prudential
Regutation Authority (PRA), as the successor to the FSA by the end of Apr!
2013, a credible plan that would restore the Bank’s capttal strength to an
appropnate level and a timeline tor delvery

Accordingly, the Bank mivated a capital planning process (the Pennine Plan)
to consider the shortfall and actions to address 1, and the plan was discussed
with the PRA during its evolution

1 Capital resources would stil have been m surplus lo {CG had the restatement delated m note 3
to the {inancuat statements been made at the time

In March 2013, the Interim Financial Policy Commuttee (FPC) of the Bank of
England recommended that the PRA should take steps to ensure that alt mayor
UK banks and building societies, moluding the Bank, held capital resources
equivalent to at least 7% of ther rsk-weighted assets (assessed using the
Basel {ll definition of equity capital) by December 2013

Ths requrement, together with the Basel jit requirement for CET1 to
remam above 4 5% at all vmes and a Leverage Ratio of 3%, were fed mto
an enhanced version of the Pennine Plan in May {an intial plan having
been submitted to the PRA as requested) The levels of expected loss mi
the corporate lending portioko and other risks were also reassessed, which
indicated that the Bank was likely to breach its ICG later 1 2013

After discussion with the PRA, this work culmmated in the Bank and The
Co-operative Group announcing on 17 June 2013 that the Bank required
additional aggregate CET1 of no tess than £1 Sbn, (of which at least £1bn
should be raised in 2013) and the Recapitalisation Plan was launched

The enhanced Pennine Plan was updated to mclude the CET1 and the non-
CET1 capital to be raised as part of the Recapitalisation Plan The resultant
plan did not expect to restore the CPB at any time in the five year planning
period, but did expect to achieve ICG complance by the end of the planning
period The required minimum CET1 ratios of 7% in normal conditions and
45% under stress were expected to be met in 2013 The completion of the
LME raised £1 2bn before costs and the 2013 year end CET1 ratio stood
at7 2%

Capital

In plain english:

Capital is held by the Bank to protect its depositors and to cover the
different nsks faced by the Bank. The minimum amount of capital the
Bank ts required to hold is set by European and national regulators

The capital requwement ts set with reference to the amount of risk the
Bank takes, defined by the amount of risk weighted assets the Bank holds
for credit risk, market risk and operational risk Credit risk represents 91%
of risk weighted assets which are calculated by weighting different types
of loan according to the potential loss that might be incurred

The amount of capital the Bank holds to meet requirements ts specified
in terms of the type of capital with specific requrements for Common
Equity Tier 1, the highest quakty of capital, as well as total Tier 1 and
total capital

As at 31 December 2013 the Basel II capital framework applied to the
Bank. The Basel Ii! capital framework came into effect from 1 January
2014 and within the European Union this has been implemented by a new
Capital Requirements Directive (CRD IV) Under Basel Ill a Leverage Ratio
has been introduced which ts a simpler measure of Bank capital levels
and ts based on the amount of good quality capiial (Tier 1) compared to
the overall balance sheet of the Bank.

During 2019, the Bank has made progress toward improving the capital
position and reducing the overall risk profile of the Bank Completion of the
LME, together with reduction in the balance sheet and risk weighted assets,
has resulted in an improvement in the capital position, however due to the
materialisation of additional conduct risks and the costs of separation, the
Dyectors now consider that additional capital will be required

Overview Acompany's credit rating is an indication of a company's

The focus of the funding and liquidity strategy of the Bank has been to

* Reduce the holding of mvesiment grade bonds that were not eligible to

central banks It also reduced rellance on short dated secured financing
to fund such assets and in tum reduced the amount of Treasury assets

Substantially ncrease secondary liquidity by mcreasing the availability

* Enter into secured private bilateral facihtes to mibgate the loss of creckt

» Maintain ratings of secured funding programmes, by the implementation

Credit rating

int May 2013, Moody s downgraded the Bank's long term credit rating by sx

long term credit rating by four notches from Ba3 to Caat In addition, m June
2013 Fitch downgraded the Bank's long term credit rating by three notches

The credit rating downgrades have

| Jed to sub-investment grade ratings on the Bank’s senior debt m
tum leading to a significant reduction in the demand for these types
of mstrument,

i negatiwely impacted the Bank's ability to access short term unsecured
wholesale funding, and

i increased the Bank's collateral requirements within some
clearing systems

On 14 November 2013, Fitch further downgraded the Bank from BB- to B
This did not have a matenal impact on the Bank as the current low credit
rating has reduced the deposit base's sensitivity to rating changes

Outlook

Non-adjusting post balance sheet events occurred between 31 December 2013 and the date of
approval of these Financial Statements These are discussed in detail in note 42 on page 219.

There is a significant task ahead to turn the Bank around, however, the management team believes
the plan wil deliver a sustainable improvement in performance over time Although the completion of
the LME improved our capital position, as announced on 24 March 2014 additional capital is required
and the raising of this CET1 is in progress The Board ts stil considering listing the Bank on the London
Stock Exchange but the timing of such a listng remains uncertain as a result of a number of factors
including the conclusions of the ongoing regulatory reviews

Our plan is to serve the needs of individuals and SMEs where we have heritage and strong customer
relationships. We are focused on delivering the strategy to be a smaller, efficient bank distinguished
by our values and ethics

Looking forward, the short to medium term outlook for the Bank remains challenging The Bank ts
dependent on the macroeconomic environment and competitive landscape in which it operates, as well
as the effective execution of the objectives that underpin the strategy such as continued deleveraging
of Non-core assets, re-pncing, investment in IT and strong cost management The Bank also needs

to attract and retain appropnately skilled peopte. In parallel, the regulatory and other investigations
announced in 2013 and 2014 are likely to subject the Bank to greater scrutiny from regulators

and the media, which could cause reputational damage and potentially distract management and

take resources away from the implementation of the strategy The most significant of the risks and
challenges the Bank faces are summanised in the Principal Risks and Uncertainties section of this
Annual Report and Accounts

Detailed financial review continued

An update on capital raising

As announced on 24 March 2014, the Bank is in the process of raising £400m
additional Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1). Having appointed advisors to assist in the
proposed CET1 raising, the Bank is now engaged in constructive discussions with
holders of a majority of the Bank’s share capital (including The Co-operative Group).
These shareholders are supportive of the planned £400m capital raise, subject to
the final structure and timing of the capital raising being agreed.

In December 2013, the Liability Management Exercise was completed, without which
the Bank would not be here. 2013 results show a loss before tax of £1.3bn before the
profit of £688m arising from the LME. While the Bank’s undertying performance in

the second half of 2013 was in line with management's expectations, the Bank has
incurred significant charges through the year and greater than expected in H2 2013,
related to, inter alia, provisions for potential additional costs of customer redress, costs
relating to breaches of the Consumer Credit Act (GCA) and additional losses relating to
the write-off of deferred tax assets.

Absent additional CET1, the Bank may fall below the current regulatory expectation of
7% GET1 ratio in the near term. Accordingly, the Board is proposing to raise additional
CET1 in the coming months. This is in addition to the remaining £263m' of the £333m

2014 contribution due from The Co-operative Group on which we remain dependent.

Successful completion of the proposed capital raising remains subject to a number of
uncertainties. These include finalisation of transaction structure, obtaining necessary
Board, regulatory and shareholder approvals, the finalisation and execution of definitive
documentation and prevailing market conditions. Accordingly, there cannot be certainty
that the proposed CET1 raising will be completed when planned or needed, on favourable
terms, or at all. The completion of this transaction is a material uncertainty as to the
going concern status of the Bank.

untformed fashion across the business

most risks which could cause the Bank’s future results, fmancial condition
and prospects to differ matenally The Bank uses its Risk Management
Framework to capture, model and articulate a risk appetite that generates
an internal view of both capital and bquidiy requirements for the Bank

currently held

Corporate Governance report continued

Compliance with the UK Corporate
Governance Code

The UK Corporate Governance Code, published by the Financial Reporting
Council in September 2012 (the Code), sets out standards of good practice

in relation to Board ieadership and effectiveness, remuneration, accountability
and relations with shareholders

Until 20 December 2013, the Bank was a wholly owned subsidiary of The
Co-operative Banking Group, which in turn was a wholly owned subsidiary
of the Co-operative Group Limited The Co-operative Group Limited was

the ulumate holding entity of The Co-operatve Banking Group (CBG) and its
subsidianes CBG maintained a common governance, organisational and risk
management structure for its subsidiaries, including the Bank. in particular,
CBG operated a common Board and set of Board Committees to oversee

the business of its subsidiaries, includmg the Bank During the second half of
2013, the Bank began putting in place its own Board and Board Committees

Role and responsibilities of the Board

The Board has collective responsitality for the long term success of the Bank
ts role 1s to provide leadership of the Bank with a framework of prudent
and effective controls which enables nsk to be assessed and managed

It sets the Bank’s values and standards and ensures that its obligations to its
shareholders, customers and other stakeholders are understood and ret

The Board sets the Bank's strategy and approves plans presented by
management for the achievement of the strategic objectives i! has set

It determines the nature and extent of the significant nsks if 1s willing to take wm
acheving its strategic objectives and ts responsible for ensuring maintenance
of sound risk management and intemal control systems

in October 2013, the Board approved a schedule of Matters Reserved for

the Board and authonties delegated to the Chief Executive Officer (CEO)

and disposals, significant investments and other capital expenditure, and
approval of the Bank's nsk appetite and its Risk Management Framework

Implementation of the strategy set by the Board and the management and

of the Matters Reserved for the Board

Co-existence Principles and the use
of The ‘Co-op’ brand

The Bank owns the trademark rights to the Co-operative Bank brand

The Co-existence Principles govern the use of trademarks contaming
‘Co-operative’ or ‘Co-op’ and other associated trademarks owned by both
Parties These principles are as follows.

Untl a Trigger Event (namely the removal of the Values and Ethics provisions
entrenched in the Bank's Articles of Association) The Co-operative Group
cannot conduct any business under a brand that combines the words
‘Co-operative’ or ‘Co-op’ and ‘Bank’ Alter the Relationstup Agreement

(see below) ends and untl a Thgger Event, the Bank can continue to use the
terms ‘Co-operative’ and ‘Co-op’ provided it does nat confuse the public ito
believing that the Bank 1s still a part of The Co-operative Group After the
expry of the non-compete penod of three years after the Relabonship
Agreement ends, The Co-operative Group is free to conduct any financra!
services business under any of its brands, subject to the restriction placed
on its use of ‘Co-operative’ or ‘Co-op’ with ‘Bank’ Unul five years after the
end of the non-compete penod, The Co-operative Group cannot conduct
any busmess which competes with the Bank under a main business brand
incluchng the words ‘Co-operative’ or Co-op"

Furthermore, the Secretary of State for Business, Innovation and Skills
tay direct the Bank to change its registered name tf, in his opmion, tt gives
$0 misleading an indication of the nature of its actues as to be kkely to
cause harm to the public The FCA has the power to prevent the use of the
‘Co-operative’ name, or to take other action regarding the Bank's branding,
if the FCA considers this desirable to protect consumers, to promote
competition in the interests of consumers or to protect the mtegrity of the
UK financial system

Relationship Agreement

In anscapation of the completion of the LIME and the Bank ceasing to be a
wholly-owned subsidiary of The Co-operative Group, the Bank entered into
a relationship agreement with The Co-operative Group and Banking Group
on 4 Novernber 2013 (Relatonstip Agreement) to regulate the basis of their
on-going relationship The Relationship Agreement wil conunue for so long
as The Co-operative Group remains a significant shareholder of the Bank

fie the nghi to exercise, or control the exercrse of, 20% of the voting nights
ata general meeting) It nchides appropriate measures regarding the degree
of control that The Co-operative Group and its associates may exercise over
management of the Bank

Under the Relationship Agreement, The Co-operative Group undertakes that
It wall conduct transactions with the Bank on arm's length terms and not seek
to influence the day-to-day running of the Bank Amendments to exsting
contracts and arrangements m place between The Co-operative Group and
the Bank will require the approval of a majority of independent Directors

The Relationship Agreement contains provisions on conflicts and related
party transactions

The Relationship Agreement requires the Bank to have a Values and Ethics
Committee which is required to

© recommend to the Board for approval and adoption the Co-operative and
ethical policies of the Bank, and

© represent, monitor and advise the Board on matters concerning the
interests of customers, suppliers, employees and other stakeholders in
their dealings with the Bank

Under the Bank's Articles of Association, there is a requirement to promote
and conduct its business to the extent practicable in a manner informed by
the established values of The Co-operatwe Movement having regard to

how tt relates to, communicates with, balances the interests of, and
otherwise deals with its stakeholders, and

« how it applies the profits of the Bank, in accordance with the dividend
policy set out m its Articles of Association

The Co-operative Group has the nght to appoint and remove

* up to two persons to the Board provided that The Co-operative Group
controls directly or mdirectly 25% or more of the vobng nights exercisable
ata general meeting of the Bank, or

* one person to the Board provided that The Co-operative Group controls
daectly or indirectly 20% or more, but less than 25% of the voting nghts
exercisable at a general meeting of the Bank. The Co-operative Group
Limited has the ability to appomt one of these Drectors to the Values and
Ethics Committee

Separation

Prior to the LME, certain functions of the Bank were centralised and carried
out by The Co-operative Group Project Unity and CFSMS Arrangements
are ihe two key arrangements in place under which the Bank receives

the supply of services, assets and/or personnel Two key agreements in
connection with Project Unity are the existing IT Services Agreement and a
Professional Master Services Agreement m relation to the provision of other
serces CFSMS was established as a direct subsidiary of The Co-operative
Banking Group in 2005 to enabie economies of scale through the sharing
of employees and sourcing of third party services across The Co-operative
Banking Group, including the Bank

Foliowing the announcement of the Bank's Recapitalisation Plan in June 2013,
the Bank and The Co-operative Group worked to amend certain provisions of
Project Unity and CFSMS Arrangements Those amendments were close to
finaksation when The Co-operative Group announced on 21 October 2013
that It was in discussion with bondholders, and that many elements of the Plan
would need to change in material respects to reflect the fact that the Bank
would no longer be a subsidiary of The Co-operative Group Accordingly the
Bank and The Co-operative Group have been working towards renegotiation
of the arrangements under which the Bank recerves staff and services from
The Co-operative Group Pending finalisation of the revised arrangements, the
Bank and The Co-operative Group agreed high level pnnciples (the Separation
Principles) to govern their separation discussions

The Separation Principles include an overarching agreement to act in good
faith and, amongst other things

© until altemative arrangements for Project Unity and the CFSMS
Arrangements are agreed, The Co-operative Group will continue to
provide services to the Bank under the same terms that such services are
currently provided,

© for those costs allocated by The Co-operative Group m its sole discretion,
the Bank and The Co-operative Group will take reasonable steps to avoid
the Bank incurring costs inctemental to those tt currently incurs,

* The Co-operative Group and the Bank will mitigate costs ansing for
The Co-operative Group or the Bank from (i) The Co-operative Group
being a less than 50% shareholder of the Bank or (ii) agreed steps taken
in connection with the separation process,

* an additional mark up (Of no more than 20%) may be charged by The
Co-operative Group for services provided under Project Unity after the
LME but not m respect of the first 12 months in respect of all services,

© costs will be allocated between the Bank and The Go-operatwe Group
on an equitable basis, and

* a working group consisting of representatives from the Bank and
The Co-operative Group will be established to address and oversee the
separation work-stream

For further mformation on the separation of the Bank and The Co-operative
Group, refer to note 39

© pay pensions or other benefits to any Director or employee,

* increase its share capital by allotting new shares,

= subdivide or consolidate and divide all or any of its share caprtal,

 redenominate all or any of its shares and reduce its share capital in

© ‘ssue redeemable shares, and
® purchase all or any of its own shares including any redeemable shares

ito shares

Significant accounting matters

Dunng the year the Committee considered the following significant accounting
issues, matters and judgements in relation to the Bank’s half year and full
year 2013 financial statements as well as the disclosure and presentation of
those areas in the Annual Report and Accounts These were discussed and
debated wath both the external auditors (KPMG Audit Plc} and management

Going concern

The continuing operation of the Bank and the Company 1s dependent

on successfully funding ther respective balance sheets and maintaining
adequate levels of capital In order to sattsfy themselves that the Bank and the
Company have adequate resources to continue to operate for the foreseeable
future, the Directors have considered a number of factors The assessment
of the going concern basis of preparation has been subject to a thorough
process involving analysis and discussion by management, Executive and
Board Committees and the Board, in Ine with our governance processes and
discussion with the PRA This analysis covered forecast nformation for the
Bank's planning period, 2014-2018, with a particular focus on the penod up
to and nckdig 2015, as well as the management actions agreed as part of
the Recapitalisation Plan {as defined and detailed mn the Strategic report

The forecasts covering the planning period included stressed scenarios,
which the Board considered to be reasonable and appropnate

The Bank has reaffirmed and updated its Plan and has continued its
engagement with the PRA in relation to this Plant There continues to be

a matenal uncertainty around the Going Concem situation of the Bank.
Further information can be found in note 1 of the Bank financial statements

Deferred tax assets

Aligned with the above going concem consideration, the Committee
deliberated and challenged the recognition of a deferred tax asset (DTA),
the key judgement being the Bank's ability to recover the deferred tax assets
based on forecasts of future taxable profits The Committee debated how
many years could be reasonably projected and agreed that the length of
the Board approved plan was the longest penod that should be considered
The Committee challenged the Plan and sensitmities to the Plan including
credit impairment levels and economic drivers. This was a key area of
discussion for the Committee during 2013 The DTA was not recognised
in the unaudited interim results as the Plan was not considered sufficiently
robust at that point In the audited interim accounts included as part of

the Prospectus, the Plans were considered robust enough to recognise a
DTA Following additional losses imcluded in the 2013 accounts and the
tequirement for additional capital to be rarsed, the Committee concluded
that a DTA could not be recognised at the 2013 year end Faflowing a
successful capital raise, this judgement will continue to be reviewed

Impairment of intangible assets

The Audit Committee discussed with management and the external

auditor the valuation of intangible assets The wnte-off of the Core banking
replacement asset was a chrect consequence of a change wn IT strategy
agreed by the Board in Q2 2013 Intangible assets on the 2013 balance sheet
include the FD online system and seven day account switching The factors
supporting these valuatons and levels of imparment, cluding customer
fumbers and switching rates, were debated by the Audit Committee

CFSMS assets

The Committee considered whether the intangible and tangible assets solely
used by the Bank but held on the balance sheet of CFSMS (previously a
fellow subsidiary of The Bantung Group) should, under accounting standards
‘risk and rewards’ test, in fact be held on the balance sheet of the Bank
Following debate and challenge covering legal ownership, the historical
position and other factors, the Directors concluded that the preferable
accounting treatment would be to include these assets in the Bank's 2013
balance sheet and restate prior years

Committee considered in the year Firsify whether the accounting of the
Bank's participation in the Pace defined benefit scheme post separation from
The Co-operative Group should be on a defined contribution defined benefit
basis After debate, the Committee concurred with management that the
Information is not currently available to account on a OB basis

The second judgement concerned the Britannia pension scheme, heid on
the balance sheet of CFSMS The Committee discussed and agreed with
management that the pension scheme was correctly accounted for in the
books of CFSMS

Turnbull review of effectiveness of
internal contro!

In the course of 2013 certan control failings became evident which prompted
further investigation and in ine wath the Code and the supporting Turnibutl
guidance, the Directors have undertaken a more systematic revew of the
effectiveness of the Bank’s system of internal control This review covered

ail matenal controls, ncluding financial, operational and complance controls
and nek management systems The assessment approach was aligned

to the Bank’s overarching Risk Management Framework (RMF) and the
effectiveness of the constituent control frameworks designed to manage the
Bank's Principal Risks The key conclusions of this review were as follows

« As described on pages 51 to 53, the Bank has a well-defined RMF that
sets out a hierarchy of strategies, policies and standards to support isk
based decision making and operational control The RMF was revised
and updated at the time of separation of the Bank from The Co-operatwe
Group, and ts considered to be m line with peer insktutons

There were changes in senior leadership during 2013 and the strategic
Imperative was to recapitalise the Bank and restructure it mto Core and
Non-core components A more m depth analysis of the contro! frameworks,
conducted by new senior management, has identified a number of control
falings The leadership team 1s focused upon ensuring there are robust
controls in place to underpin the business strategy

Astronger Risk Function was established im the period of late 2012 to early
2013 There have been a number of control improvements across multiple
nsk disciplines during 2013 The challenge going forward will be to attract

new, and retain existing key staff to maintam and support transformation

of the Bank in a controlled way

Historically, the Internal Audit function did not meet the standards recently
set out by the Chartered Insutute of intemal Auditors in relation to financial
services In addition, intema! audit action points were not addressed by
line management in line with agreed tmescales in 2013, a new Target
Operating Model (TOM) was implemented which significantly improved
the capabilty of the function Where progress is not being made on audit
issues, these are now routinely escalated to the Executive and Board Audit
Committees This ts part of the wider cultural changes being introduced
across the Bank

* Vanous elements of Operational Risk management were conckided
to be ineffectrve in 2013 Some examples of weaknesses include an
over-reliance on manual intervention n processes, inadequate business
continuity and disaster recovery arrangements, poor logical access
management and meffective outsourcing Progress has been achieved ina
number of areas but significant work is sull required from a people, process
and systems perspective to embed an effective and sustainable operational
risk framework, and to enhance the control culture

Significant control failures have been identified in relahon to Regulatory and
Conduct matters and, similar to other organisations, maternal provisions
have been required n 2013 Mayor efforts are being made to rectify these
falings and prevent their recurrence

* The Bank's underlying business and financial systems are dated and have
suffered from a lack of ntegration and investment since the Britannia
merger Whilst the Bank does not believe this will result in maccuracies
or delays in the financial or other information which it is required to
publish, the significant, costly manual mtervention does nevertheless
lead to an increased level of financial reporting nsk. A number of control
enhancements were made m 2013 Further nvestment and automation
1's required to maintain compliance with emerging regutatory and statutory
reporting requirements

# Internal audit fmdings and risk events reported in 2013 support the above
assessment of the Bank's system of intemal control

Alter reviewing the presentations and reporis from management and
consulung, where necessary, with the external auditors, the Committee was
satisfied that the financial statements appropnately addressed the entical
judgements and key estimates (both m respect of the amounts reported

and the disclosures) The Committee was also satisfed that the significant
assumptions used for determining the value of assets and habiltes had been:
appropriately scrutinised, challenged and were sufficiently robust

in summary, the Bank’s systems of control were weak and had failings ina
number of areas in the past. The foundations of a more robust RMF were
defined in late 2012 to early 2013, some improvements m controls were
made m1 2013 but further work is required to implement and embed all
elements of the control framework across the Bank This will need to be

a cultural shift n driving accountability for actions This 1s a corporate priority
for 2014

Risk Committee report

Introduction by Risk Committee Chairman

“The scale of the risks identified in the second half of 2013 were such that they required
full Board consideration. Consequently, the focus has been on overhauling our internal
risk controls and conduct risk procedures. The management of the Bank’s risks are
now distinct from The Co-operative Banking Group's and a separate Risk Management
Framework has been established, There is much change required and there is no doubt
that embedding the Risk Management Framework will be challenging. In addition,
there are still significant risks facing the business which could impact its ability to
implement its Plan. Our role is to ensure rigorous challenge and debate of the Bank’s
key risks, challenging management on strategic decisions and the Plan and driving
accountability amongst the senior management team.”

Graeme Hardie
Risk Committee Chairman

To review and report ts conclusions to the Board on the Bank's risk appetite and Risk Management Framework, taking a forward looking perspective and
anticipating changes in business conditions

Transfer of shares

Shareholders may transfer any shares in any manner which ts permitted by
law and 1s from time to tme approved by the Board PRA approval is required
if a person intends to acquire or increase its ‘control’ of a Ui authorised
person (which includes the Bank) Acquiring ‘control’ includes where a
person first holds 10% or more of the shares or voting power in the Bank
tncreasing contro! means when an existing shareholder increases their
sharehofding or entitlement to voting power from a holding below certain
thresholds to a holding above them The thresholds are 10%, 20%, 30%

or 50% of shares or voting power

Voting rights
Subject to the Bank's Articles of Association and to any special rights or
restrictions as to voting for the time being attached to any shares, the

provisions of the Companies Act 2006 shall apply in relation to voting rights
of the Bank's shares

Ordinary shareholders are entitled to vote at general meetings and appoint
proxies On a show of hands, every member or proxy has one vote Ona poll,
every member present in person or by proxy has one vote for every share
held Ordinary shareholders may receive (1) diadends, (2) assets upon the
liquidation of the Bank and (3) capital payments through the capitalisation

of reserves as provided in the Bank’s Articles of Association

nghts are being varied or (2) the Bank ts beng wound up or there rs other
return of capital

of the holder of the preference shares The Bank’s Articles of Association
require this consent to be obtamned for certain

Pension ~ Pace

Pace includes the defined beneiit section (Pace Complete) and the defined contnbution sectons (Pace Essential and Pace Extra} All employees can choose to
jon Pace Complete once they have completed two or more consecutive years’ service

« Pace Complete provides defined benefit pensions based on 1/60th of average pensionable earnmys, revalued by mflation for each year of pensionable service

Benefits accrued as at § April 2006, m respect of membership of the scheme preceding Pace, continue to be knked to fina! pensionable salary at a member's
date of leaving or retrerment, whichever is earber The scheme also provides pensions to dependants on death

* Members of Pace Essental, into which all elgble employees will be automatically enrotled, currently pay 1% of pay and the Group pays 2% Members of Pace
Extra, which is available to alt employees, currently pay 4% of pay and the Group pays 8%

* Under all sections a hump sum is payable if death occurs in service

* All members of Pace have the choice of making pension cantnbutons by salary sacnfice

Executive Directors who are members of Pace Complete currently contribute 8% of ther pensionable salary or 7% of pensionable salary (until October 2013)
if they were a contributing member of Pace at 31 December 2011 The Group pays the balance of the cost of previding defined benefits

Where an Executive Deector may be impacted by the Annual Allowance or the Lifetme Allowance, they have the factity of opting out of future pension accrual
in favour of a pension allowance or opting for restncted pension accrual based on a capped pensionable salary of £187,500 and recemng a restncted pension
allowance in feu of pension prousion based on salary above £187,500 Where paid, the rate of pension allowance is based on the employer contribution rate to
Pace Complete This is currently 16% of pensionable salary

Executive Directors who jon Pace Extra are provided with an additional 8% of pay as a pension contribution or pension allowance for the period they are neligible
to jon Pace Complete

Supplementary tife cover is provided to Executive Directors in order to provide total life cover of 4 x salary when aggregated with benefits from Pace,

as appropnate

Additional information

Dunng the year, long term incentives were awarcted on the basis that clawback provisions would apply to any payments, under which the Committee coukd seek
to recoup part or afl of a payment by reference to subsequent business performance

Any variable payments made to the Executwe Directors are subject to deferral in accordance with the Remuneration Code Deferred payments are subject toa
malus adjustment where the value can be adjusted downwards to take account of specified conditions for example, in an instance of an executive action which
leads to customer detriment or if there ts a matenal breach of risk management

Service contracts and policy on payment for loss of office

Its the Bank's current policy that the notice period n Executrve Directors service contracts should not exceed one year, save in the case of Niall Booker, whose
contract can be terminated by the Company on not Jess than 12 months’ notice, such notice not to expire prior to 10 June 2015 Niall Booker may terminate
his employment by the giving of 6 months’ notice to the Bank All the other Executive Directors have contacts which can be terminated by one year's notice by
either party in the event of terrnation, the standard payment due to an Executive Director would be based on the value of base salary and other contractual
benefits (in the case of Niall Booker, to include any role based allowance which may be due under the terms of his contract) Where it s deemed commercially
beneficial for an individual to remain under a contract of employment with the Bank, they are placed on garden leave and only contractual payments are made

The Committee could exercise its discretion relatng to any deferred payments due from previous variable pay awards and could reduce such deferred payments
to zero in ight of subsequent events

Pillar 3 disclosures

As part of its responsibilities, the Bank Remuneration Commitiee also oversees remuneration for individuals who are included on the Bank’s register of
‘Code Staff’ As noted within this Directors’ Remuneration Report, a new remuneration policy 1s being developed for Directors in addition and as part of the
same exercise, a new remuneration policy will be developed and adopted for Code Staff

Its intended that the new remuneration policy will be based on linking pay to performance and ensuring that any remuneration arrangements are consistent
with the Bank’s risk appetite Any variable remuneration arrangements in place will ncorporate performance conditions that support the Bank's strategy and
also comply with the provisions of the PRA Remuneration Code

The current policy operated for Cade Staff is based on the following

* Code Staff are defined as those staff having a material impact on the Bank's risk profile, including a person who performs a significant influence function for
the Bank, a senior manager and a risk taker

* Decisions on remuneration for Code Staff are made by the Committee Advice is taken from the Risk function in making remuneration decisions The design
of the component parts of remuneration Is as set out for the Executive Directors above

© For Code Staff, performance related pay is risk adjusted through the use of underpins to the incentive arrangements, together with the use of deferrais,
clawback and malus adjustments in accordance with the PRA Remuneration Code, as set out for the Executsve Drectors above

Board and sub-committees

The Bank's risk governance structure is ntended to strengthen nsk evaluation and managernent whilst ensuring the Bank manages the regulatory envronment
as efficiently as possible The nsk focus of these Committees is descnbed below

Committee

Risk focus

The Board

The responsibitites of the Board nclude

© setting the overall Bank risk strategy,

© approving the design and implementation of risk management approaches, including the Risk Management Framework and
the risk appetite statement and metrics that underpin them,

» delegating authonty for oversight of the Risk Management Framework, systems and high-level limits to the Board Risk
Committee (BRC),
* delegating authority for cascading the setting and approval of the more granular nsk hmits and tolerances to the CEO,

* using the output of the semu-annual certification process, to approve the effectiveness of the Risk Management Framework

Risk management — 2013 overview

2013 was a year of substantive change in the Risk function In conunction with redefining the Risk Management Framework (RMF) and constructing an effective
three ‘mes of defence model in the second half of 2012, the previous and present Chief Risk Officers (CROs) began a process of significantly strengthening the
capability of the Risk Leadership team and the second ne function This enhanced Risk function, which has been in place since first quarter 2013, has been
responsible for the identification of previously un-remediated policy, process and control faings The kentiftcation of these issues led to improvements across
multipte risk disciplines, most particularly in the areas of Operational, Conduct, Liquidity and Credit Risk The further identification of risks and the subsequent
improvements in policy, process and control are still ongoing

A key element in the redefined Risk Management Framework ts the reiteration that nsk ts not the responsibility of any one part of the mstrtution or group of
employees but rather the responsibilty of all This requirement, for a core risk culture driving our business, ts one of the key improvements being embeddect
across the Bank

The challenge for 2014 and beyond will be the retention of key staff to maintain and burld the capability of the function to support the simplification and
transformation of the Bank in a controlled manner, as well as a continumg focus on embedding a stronger Risk culture across the institukon

Risk Management Framework

Untt the second half of 2013, The Co-operative Banking Group maintained common govemance, organisational and nsk management structures, which had
been developect and implemented across tts subsidiaries inclucing the Bank

in 2013, the Bank put in place its own revised, updated and independent Risk Management Framework and policies, which have been adopted by the Bank
Executive and the Board The Bank’s new Risk Management Framework reflects the previous system in many respects, and continues to be refined to meet
the Bank's specific requrements

As a result of the new Risk Management Framework, the Bank has determmed its prncipal nsks, and the level of acceptable risks, as stipulated in the Bank's
nsk appebte statement, thus ensurmg that there ts an adequate system of nsk management so that the levels of capital and hquidity held are conssstent with the
nek profile of the business

The Bank has also replicated the three lines of defence’ governance model, previously adopted by the Board of Directors of The Co-operative Banking Group
This ts to ensure appropriate responsibility and accountability 1s allocated to the management, whist recognising that the system is designed to manage rather
than ekromate nsk of failure to achreve business objectives The three lines of defence are

» the Bank's ousiness teams and first Ine management act as the first line of defence and are responsible for identtymg where a business unit 1s exposed to
risks, mcluding from the development of new products, processes or other busmess change They also manage the nsks that reside withm their busmess units
on a day-to-day basis, implementing effective monitoring and control processes to ensure thal the Bank's business’ risk profile is understood and mamtamed
within the Board-defined risk appetite,

* the Bank's complance and rsk functions act as the second line of defence They oversee and challenge the implementation and monitoring of the Risk
Management Framework and consider current and emerging risks across the Bank They also revew and challenge the delegated authority framework and
oversee appropnate escalation of breaches, mitigating acbons and reporting to the Execute Risk Committee (ERC), and

® the Bank's nternal audit function acts as the third line of defence They are responsible for ndependently venfying that the principal Risk Control Framework
has been implemented as intended across the business, and independently challenge the overall management of the iramework to provde assurance to the
Board Audit Committee (BAC) and senior management on the adequacy of both the first and second Ines

Principles
To achieve the strategic goals, the following principles are mandated across the organisation

© the Board requires the business to be managed in line with the risk strategy which sets out the agreed vision within the agreed risk appetite Risk, as well
as reward, shouid be taken into account in a consistent way across the business when pursuing all strategic objectives to maintain the desired risk profile,

© the Board is ultimately responsibie for all the Bank's risks and approves limits for the business so it may operate within the agreed risk appetite The Board
expects management to realise strategic objectives whilst operating withm these limits and an effective risk culture must be in piace so these {mits are
adhered to,

an mdependent review and challenge is provided by the CRO who ts supported by an appropriate governance structure to ensure effective implementation
of the agreed Risk Management Framework,

* recognised, emerging or current risks are actively managed in line with the Bank’s approach for identification, measurement, management, monitoring and
reporting identification must be forward-looking to allow management to take a pro-active approach to risk,

* rigorous stress testing exercises are carried out to assess the impact of a range of adverse scenarios with different probabilities and severities ta inform the
Bank's strategic planning processes,

* management monitors the aggregated risk profile of the Bank and its regulated entities, and reviews trends which are reported to the Board and externally
to regulators to meet external obligations, and

© high quality risk management techniques are adopted across the business with the use of these evidenced through documentation and self-certification

Risk appetite

The Board has primary responsibility for identifying the key business risks facing the Bank, approving the Bank s risk strategy and the acceptable level of nsk
appetite and associated tolerances, and delegates the setting of the detaed risk limits and tolerances to the Chief Executive Officer (CEO) The CEO uses the
Executive Committee (ExCo) governance structure to assist im consideration, review and setting of these more detailed risk appetite limits and tolerances

The Bank's risk appetite frarmework encompasses seven key principles which are eamings volatility, capital buffer, regulatory capital adequacy, liquidity and
funding, reputation and conduct, reguiatory compliance and operational effectiveness

Each of the seven risk appetite principles is supported hy quantitative measures to ensure that the Bank operates within the expectations of key stakeholders,
Including Its customers, rating agencies and regulators

The risk appetite review process 1s aligned to the annual planning cycle, and accordingly the tolerances for each of the risk appetite measures have been
recalibrated based on the plan for Board approval, including a refresh of some measures The framework is being developed to incorporate the plans for each
of the Core and Non-core Businesses

1. Credit risk

In plain english:

Credit risk is the risk that borrowers wil fail to repay what they have borrowed The risk to the Bank mcludes the amounts lent not bemg repaid, loan interest
Not being received, cash flows being recelved later than expected, and increased collection costs This results in the need to set aside provisions and capital
to provide for future losses

1.1 Overview

Credit risk ts one of the principal risks set out in the Risk Management Framework and Is an integral part of the business activities of this and alf other banks Itis
inherent in both traditional banking products (loans, commitments to lend and contingent liabilites such as letters of credit} and in ‘traded products’ (derrvative
contracts such as forwards, swaps and options, repurchase agreements, securities borrowing and lending transactions}

The inherent risks arising from general economic conditions have been mcreased by the period of significant turbulence and uncertanty affecting the global
economy and the global financial system, and the Eurozone in particular The Bank continues to be exposed to these risks and their consequences, including
lower consumer confidence, high levels of unemployment, interest rate volatdty and increased cost of credit, which may result in increased credit risk which
could have a material adverse effect on the Bank’s business

All authority to take credit nsk derives from the Board This authority 1s delegated te the CEO who then sub-delegates to appropriate committees and individuals
The level of crecht nsk authority delegated depends on seniority and experience, and vanes according to the quality of the counterparty, associated security or
collateral held

The Bank's policy for credit risk ts required to be approved annually by the ERC and defines appropriate standards and principles for the effective management of
credrt risk throughout the Bank’s divisions

Credit risk management is an essential element of the Bank's operations As with all principal risks, the Board requires that the credit business is managed in ine
with the sk strategy and risk appetite set by the Board Risk measurement is based on a set of metrics, which are aligned with the Board agreed risk appetite
and support the limits framework These metrics undergo penocic review to assure that they are fit for purpose, le, are able to recognise both emerging and
current risks Crecit risk models are subject to biennial review by the Model Risk Forum

In May 2013, in order to strengthen internal risk controls, the Bank Executive, with the approval of the Board Risk Committee and the Board, moved all corporate
lending authority to the three principle Executive Lending Committees, the LCC, SCC and STC (see Executive and Management committees section) This has
proven especially beneficial during the restructuring of this portfolio into that of CoAM and BaCB as new underwriting procedure for these businesses are

being formutated

The Credit Risk Management Forum (CRMF) considers key management information to support the oversight and challenge of the credit risk embedded in each
division and across the credit risk life cycle This includes appropriate benchmarking information from similar portfolios in the market and 1s key to calibrating
risk appetite

The CRMF ts supported by an additional three forums that exist to support the Credit Risk Director in discharging the second line mandate These include an
{mnpairment Charge Forum, implemented in June 2013, which was designed to ensure that the credit risk Impasrment and loss recognition policy standard is
being imptemented effectively and that there is sufficient evidence to support the credit risk impairment stock In addition, an Impament Adequacy Forum
was implemented in the second haf of 2013 as a mechanism for approving and reviewing the refresh of mpamment parameters and post model adjustments
The third forum is the Treasury Credit Risk Management Forum which provides day-to-day oversight of the credit risk exposure in the Treasury division

The retail division of the Core Bank uses both applicaton and behavioural sconng techniques to rank a customer's risk of defauit with this, in common with
other retail banks, being embedded in the business The Non-core diviston, which includes corporate lending, cludes a number of specialist models to reflect
the embedded credit risk of sectors such as private finance initiatives (PFls} and commercial property The performance of all rating systems is govemed by the
IRB Mode! Working Group with oversight from a new Model Risk Forum that met for the first time dunng the third quarter of 2013

Fair value adjustments

At the ume of the merger of Britannia Building Society with the Bank, all of the assets and liabilities of Britannia were acquired at their then estimated fair values
Valuations of Britannia's assets included adjustments made for thew lifetime future expected losses at that date, referred to as the credit fair value adjustments
Previously, the credit fair value adjustments have been disclosed separately in the above table, the table has now been revised to set off the credit fair value
adjustments within the not impaired and impaired gross balances, to betier show thes gross carrying values m the Bank

For the purposes of internat management reporting of coverage ratios, loss provisions are grossed up by the amount of remaining credit fair value adjustments
and compared to the gross amounts owed by customers, as shown in section 1 3 Credit risk management

future recovery, discounted at the loan’s effective terest rate, taking nto account the expected charge off rate and any supporting collateral

1.3.1.1 Secured residential credit risk

Acquisition and account management

Mortgages are loans to customers secured by a first charge over a residential property Mortgages are originated directly to customers via branches, telephone
and the mtemet under the Britannia and Co-operative Bank brands, and via intermediaries under the Platform brand In the year to 31 December 2013, 66%
{2012 70%) of mortgages were originated directly and 34% (2012 30%) through mtermedianies

The Britannua and Co-operative Bank brands only originate prime residential mortgages, white Platform primarily onginates a combination of prime residential,
almost prme residential and buy-to-let loans

Hestoriwally, these loans have been advanced on a capital and interest payment basis, where the loan ts repaid over the term of the loan, or interest only, where
the capital element of the loan is repayable at the end of the term All new advances are on a capital repayment basis, with the exception of buy-to-let lending
and existing interest only customers moving home

Durng the term of the mortgage, interest only mortgages are managed consistently with capital and repayment mortgages, except for our customer contact
svategy For interest only mortgages this aims to determme if the customer has a satisfactory repayment strategy in place upon loan maturity

Impairment

Where objective evdence of impairment has been observed as a result of one or more loss events, impairment held at the balance sheet date is defined as
identified impairment

Loans under Forbearance are sulyect to a specific identified smparment assessment

Loans where no objective evidence has been observed are collectively assessed for unidentified impamment (ie where a loss event has occurred, but objective
evidence of impairment has not yet manifested)

Loans meeting the individually Significant asset definition (large value loans which exceed a balance threshald established by the Bank) are individually assessed
for Imparment

We separate certain loans into discrete pools for the assessment of potentaal impairment, which has not been specifically dentdied but which we know has
occurred due to specific conditions which we have observed These are

* those loans which are in arrears by less than one monthly instalment, and
© those loans where there has been a material deterioration m the borrower's external credit score in the last 12 months

For each joan assessed for impairment, whether identified or undentified, a shortlall s calculated based on the difference between the current loan balance and
the expected ‘forced sale’ price of the collateral, discounted at the effactve mterest rate of the loan to reflect the anticipated time to sale, and takmg into account
anticipated fees and costs prior to sale

The shortfall 1s multipked by the probability the oan will defauft and further multiplied by the probability that the loan will be taken mto possession to determine
the impairment required

Forbearance

Forbearance occurs when, for reasons relating to actual or apparent financial difficulty of the borrower, a temporary or permanent concession ts granted
A concession may involve short term restructuring of the payment terms of the loan or an extension of the maturity date

The primary aim of forbearance Is to help the borrower through a temporary period of financial difficulty and retum the account into @ sustainable position where
the facilty can be serviced through to full repayment Where the primary am cannot be achieved, the secondary aim ts to maximise recovery of debt in a manner
which ts affordable to the borrower

A number of options are available to borrowers in financial difficulty Repossession of the property will only be considered when all other avenues have been
explored The precise treatment selected depends on the borrower's ndividual crcumstances, but may mclude

© arrangements to repay outstanding arrears over a period of time, by making payments above the contractual amount The loan ts deemed to be impaired until
the arrears have been cleared in full,

* shori term concessions, where the borrower is allowed to make reduced repayments (or, in exceptional circumstances, no fepayments) on a temporary basis
to assist with a short term financial hardship In these cases the shortfall on the repayments will accrue as arrears and the oan ts deemed to be impaired,

whilst permanent or temporary conversion io interest only repayments Is no longer allowed, where this has been agreed in the past two years, a loan is
considered to have been subject to forbearance,

© an extension to the morigage term to reduce the borrower's repayments Alt oans that have had a term extension in the jast two years are considered to be
subject to forbearance, even where these were not agreed as a forbearance treatment,

© capitalisation, where outstanding arrears are added to the capital value of the loan to be repaid over the remaining term This is only considered by exception,
with nine cases capitalised during the year to 31 December 2013 (2012 20), and

© where applicable we will work with the customer as part of a goverment support scheme In this case, unmet loan payments will continue to accrue as
arrears until such time as the loan 1s cleared, or the outstanding payments are brought up to date

Payment holidays are allowed as part of a customer's fleable mortgage contract Payment holdays are not knowingly allowed for customers experiencing
financial difficulty

Mortgages subject to forbearance are assessed to determine which is the appropriate impamment treatment to apply

1.3.1.3 Corporate credit risk

Acquisition and account management

The Bank operates to a lending policy which ts subject to meeting criteria as laid dowm in the corresponding sector strategy guidelines and strict policy with
regards to single name and sector concentrations First line relatonship managers submit recommendations for any new money, renewal of facilities or
amendments to existing lending to the appropriate sanctioning authority The supporting credit underwriting team, who are independent from income generation,
will review and risk assess the proposal before submitting their recommendation to the sanctioning authority for approval The credit underwriting team uses
rating systems and other tools to analyse the underlying business and the counterparty's management io assess the nsk of each lending proposition and its
ability to repay the borrowing Other factors considered mclude the potential impact of economic changes, availability of supporting collateral, the financial
stability of the counterparty and its abikty to withstand such change

The lending discretions for new money are made by the LCC (mainly Non-core customers due to their size), the Strategic Asset Renew (SAR) groups (Non-core
only) or the SCC (Core business) The CRO, the CFO and the CEO are members of the LCC The CoAM strategy 1s executed through higher risk (default and
watchlist) and performing SAR groups comprising of first and second line business and credit risk representatives where the majority of decisions are taken to
set run-off or exit strategies, (on occasion these strategies do require additional tending to protect value in the work out of a customer asset} Beyond certain ;
tevels of exposure and/or new money, the SAR groups make recommendations to the LCC for the fund decision

Monitoring of portfolios and impairment provisionmg governance 1s in place across the portfolios Individual cases which show signs of unsatisfactory
performance are managed through a specialist Corporate Business Support team who are engaged with the customers to restore them to good health or,
wherever this is not possible, take actions to effect recovery

In addition to the first lne Operating Committees for BaCB and CoAM, the CRMF and ERC {and by exception the BRC) receive regular reports on the
performance of the portfolio

Impairment

Corporate customers are placed on a watchhst and treated as impaired when they show signs of unsatistactory performance and require close monitoring
Strong indicators that a customer should be placed on watchlist include but are not limited to

* unsatisfactory account operation ~ both lencing facility and current accounts,

* considerable reduction in value of collateral,

* deteriorating balance sheet position and/or material losses/cashtlow pressures,

* breach of covenants,

 relinance risk at matunty,

© application of forbearance strategies, and

business performance 1s assessed to have detenorated to the extent that there is a real nsk of loss of pnncpal, interest or fees

After a penod of satisfactory performance, and dependent on the Bank's agreement that the triggers wich led to mclusion on the watchhst no longer apply,
customers may be removed from the watchlist

1. Credit risk continued

Removal from the watchiist usually requires the customer's management information to show that trading and account performance is achieving the revised plan
with objective evidence that thes will continue

The Bank also reviews on a monthly basis, through the Impairment Charge Forum, ali higher risk loans (including those accounts subject to forbearance) and
considers the potential loss which might arise were the borrowers to fail (notwithstanding that contnued trading remains the expectation)

Objective evidence of impairment will include out ts not limited to
© an instalment on a loan account being overdue, or having been in excess of its limit (or being overdrawn without an agreed limit) for 90 days or more,
* an event likely to result in nsolvency which may involve bankruptcy, or the appointment of an admunistrative receiver, hquidator or adrunstrator, or

* if the Bank considers that at some point (normally within the next 12 months} the facility is unlikely to be repaid in full and actions such as an issue of formal
demand will be required in order to achieve full repayment

A credit nsk impairment charge ss required on some of of the entire shortfall between the secunty held and the loan balance outstanding and represents an
assessment of the likely net loss after realisation of any security

For credit risk impairment purposes, a property valuation or selling agent’s recommendation is discounted to take into account selling and legal costs and also to
butd m a contingency to cover potential reductions in the selling price based upon the type of secunty and entity and the existence or otherwise of a contracted
sale In some cases, calculation of the credit nsk impawment is based on an up to date assessment (often following an independent business review by a firm

of accountants) of likely recetvables from the business or a formal estimated outcome statement from an insolvency practitioner where the business has fated

Provisioning adjustments are also recorded, as appropriate, against loans whose mterest terms have changed such that revised future cash flows discounted
at the orginal nterest rate are less than the current carrying amount

The collective unidenbfied impairment provision (CUIP} provides cover for the performing portfolio using a formutarc approach based on default expenence
across the portfolio The unidentified mparment reflects trigger events that have occurred but not yet been klentfied and cannot therefore be allocated to
dividual loans

Ifthe Bank 1s confident of a customer's abiity and commitment to address their financial difficulues, it may agree fo grant concessions to the orginal contractual

terms Such concessions typically include

* restructuring, waming or reserving rights un the event of covenant breaches,

* postponement of pnncipal payments,

* restructures of principal payments,

* extension of loan maturites,

* partial or full capitalsation of interest payments, or
* swap restructures

In addition to the above, other forbearance concessions are considered to be obyective evidence of mparment and include
© a partal write-off of debt, following which the account continues to be classified as impaired for at least sx months, or
* a maternal postponement or forgiveness of mterest or ‘soft’ rates or warver or reduction of normal fees and charges, the accounts must remain impaired while

such favourable terms are bemy appled
