2. Liquidity risk Unaudited

tn plain english:

Liquidity and funding nsk is the nsk that the Bank cannot pay amounts that it owes when they fall due, or can only do so at excessive cost The risk can arise
either because

© the Bank has insufficient funds to pay the amounts it owes, or
© even though the Bank has sufficient funds, those funds have been invested and so are not readily available

The Bank's business faces inherent risks concerning liquidity, particutarly i the availability of traditional sources of funding such as retail and corporate deposits
and, to a lesser extent, unsecured whelesale funding (these bemg the principal sources of the Bank's funding) become limited and/or more expensive This may
result in an inability to operate in ihe ordinary course, and/or a failure te meet liquidity requirements, and/or may acversely impact the Bank's business and/or the
implementation of its strategy

As the Bank’s main source of funds 1s deposits, the Bank faces the nsk of not being able to replace funds when they are withdrawn or, if a significant nurnber of
depositors withdraw thei funds, of not being able to meet its obhgations to fund such withdrawals, repay lenders i accordance with tts financing arrangements
or tulfif commitments to lend However, despite the steps that the Bank has taken to maintam its levels of total liquidity, as the Bank rehes on its customer
deposits to provide funding for the Bank, any severe decline in customer confidence in the Bank could mcrease the amount of deposit withdrawals in a short
space of ime of over a sustained period

Given the relative swe of the Bank’s deposit base as compared with its other sources of funding, the Bank is particularly exposed to any serious loss of
confidence by its depositors If the Bank expenences an unusually high Jevel of withdrawals which exceed what the Bank can manage through liquidity controls
and contingency planning, this may have a negative effect on the Bank’s business and coutd, mn extreme crcumstanices, prevent the Bank from meeting tts
financial abhgations as they fall due, meeting its regulatory minimum jiquidity requirements or fulfilling sts comnmmutments to lend in these crcumstances, the Bank
Tay be subject to a resolution procedure under the Banlung Act

in the year to 31 December 2013, however, the Bank has contmued to maintain a sufficiently stable kquidity position, with a liquid asset ratio of 16 0% at the
balance sheet date (2012 146%) In addition, dunng 2073, the Bank proactively managed its iquidity position, maintain a regulatory liquidity buffer, and has
continued to restructure existing exposures to further improve the funding profile and ensure its financial obligations are met as and when they fall due From a
management perspective, liquidity is monitored on a daily basis via a sunte of liquidity nsk metrics supported by cash flow forecasts and stress tested forecasts
2.1 Liquidity framework Unaudited

The Bank's tiquicity Risk Management Framework compres

* a defined risk appetite, controls and governance in the Bank's liquidity management policies,

* articulation of how hquidity risk 1s identified, measured, monitored and managed in the Inchvidual Liquidity Adequacy Assessment (ILAA) and procedures and
governance in place to mitigate the risk,

© Iqquidity risk quantification and mitigation techniques and processes,

* management actons kinked through to stress testing and liquidity planning models, enablmg a method of mitigating the effects of a number of stress scenarios
for varying periods of ume and to ensure that the Bank operates within its agreed risk appetite parameters in all planning models,

* ongoing development and enhancement of the Bank's liquidity risk appetite framework, and
© a process to attribute the cost, benefit and risks of liquidity to specific business Ines wa the Bank's funds transfer pricing mechanism

2.2 Liquidity risk Unaudited

The Board's nsk appetite for liquidity nsk is defined mn terms of

* surwval periods which measure the ability of liquid assets to support the Bank's actmity over time under a number of stress scenarios,
adherence to strategic liquidity nsk measures, and

* compliance with all regulatory Iiquidity nsk limits

The stress tests encompass survival across various timescales and a range of adverse liquidity events, both firm specific and market wide, which endeavour
to cover all aspects of the liquidity risk to which the Bank is exposed

The strategic measures appraved by the Board include

* customer loan/deposit ratio, 95% (2012 92%) — the ratio of gross toans and advances to customers to total customer deposits,
* encumbrance ratio, 27% (2012 27%) ~ the ratio of encumbered assets divided by total assets,

* regulatory framework — Net Stable Funding ratio and Internal Liquidity Guidance, and

© internal liquidity stress tests — the survival period of the Bank under a range of stressed scenarios

3. Market risk

In plain english:

Market nsk is the nsk of losses anging due to changes n market pnces The Bank's biggest exposure to market nek is from changes in mterest rates
The Bank also has a small exposure to market nek from changes in foreign currency exchange rates

Market nsk is the risk of logs as a result of the value of financial assets or lattes (including off-balance sheet mstruments} being adversely affected by
movements in market rates or prices This loss can be reflected in the near term earnmgs by changing net interest imcome, or in the longer term because of
changes i the ecanorme vatue of future cash flaws

The main source of market nsk within the Bank s driven by mismatches between the repricing profiles of asset and lability customer products within the Retail
and Corporate businesses and certain charactenstics embedded within these products and basts risk Treasury also creates market nsk through ts various
portfolio management acttes along with currency nk.

3.1 Interest rate risk

Interest rate nsk policy statements, approved by the ERC on behalf of the Board, specify the scope of the Bank's wholesale market activity, market rsk limits
and delegated authories The policy is managed by the Bank Market Risk Forum (BMRF) and ALCO Them prime task is to assess the interest rate risk mherent
in the maturity and repricing charactenstics of the Bank's assets and labilittes The Bank seeks to minimise the volatility of future earnings from terest rate
changes and all nterest rate nsk exposure ts removed from the Core and Non-core divisions and consolidated at the centre where itis managed from the Core
balance sheet within agreed timits Treasury is responsible for interest rate risk management for the Bank The principal analytical techniques involve assessing
the impact of different interest rate scenarios and changes in balances over various time periods

The Board recerves reports on the management of balance sheet nsk and BMRF and ALCO revew the balance sheet risk positions and the uthhsation of
wholesale market risk mits

3.1.1 Non-treasury interest rate risk

The Bank (excluding wholesale treasury risk) uses a PVO1, gap report and earings approach for managing interest rate risk, focusing in detail on the sensitivity
of assumed changes in interest rates on net interest income for one year

BMRF monitors the non-trading interest rate risk, which Is split between certain wholesale portfolios, banking and investment books, and the rest of the Bank's
balance sheet The following describes the Bank non-trading portfolios, excluding these certain wholesale portfolios (These positions are managed by Treasury }
All interest rate nsk 1s centralised into Treasury using appropriate transfer pricing rates

Gap reports are based on defined time periods ALCO sets guidance fimits around the gap, principally that the sum of positions maturing in greater than
12 months and non-sensitive balances (includes non-maturity deposits) are no more than a set limit

Non maturity deposits, which are non-interest bearing, are separated into a stable ‘core’ element, based on a long sun average, and the residual balance, which
can fluctuate In the gap report, the residual balance (along with interest bearing non-maturity deposits) is deemed to re-price or mature within one month The
‘core’ non-maturity deposits are with the non-sensitive balance on the gap report, along with non-dated capital and other non-sensitive balances ALCO sets
gutdance around the treatment of non-sensitive balances to remvest in fixed rate assets in periods up to five years to smooth the mcome based upon the
prevailing interest rate environment

Risk limits are formally calculated at each month end Interest rate risk and effectiveness of hedging is monitored daily using gap positions, incorporating new
business requirements Draw down nsk, in particular for fixed rate mortgages, is managed through weekly balance sheet meetings The asset and liability
management team undertakes hedges for mterest rate risk using denvatve struments and invesiment secuntes which are executed wa the Treasury markets
team to external wholesale markets, and loans and deposits which are executed intemally with the Treasury markets team

Basis nek is the nek that different assets and habiities re-price with reference to different indices and at different times. This exposes the Bank to income volatility
if mdices do not move ina ratio of one toone The overall exposure to basis risk has remained a net base rate asset throughout 2013 as customers continue to
favour vanable rate mortgages (where the introductory rate 1s inked to Bank of England base rate) and a large proportion of the liquidity of the Bank ts placed m
the Bank of England reserve account Basis nsk is monitored by BMRF and ALCO monthly and action is taken as required, which includes peeing, new products
or extemal hedging

The table illustrates the greater than 12 month net gap position at the end of the period on the Bank's balances, excluding wholesale treasury and customer
currency balances which are managed within the Treasury risk framework The gap is driven by product pricing and product mix The gap ts calculated by
placing all assets and llabilities at the earlest of ther re-pricing or matunty date and then summing by time band The aim s to have assets evenly spread so
that the Bank 1s not exposed to sudden rate movements The net position shows the amount that the Bank is either over or under invested at a postin time
A£100m positive gap position would equate to the Bank's income mereasing by £1m per annum ff rates increased by 1% The maximum sensitity for the
period shawn below equates to approximately an £8 Om (2012 £10 9m) decrease n income if rates mcreased by 1%

3.1.2 Treasury interest rate nsk Audited

Treasury manage the Bank’s liquid asset portfolios and executes derivative wstuments with the wholesale markets on behalf of the Bank and Its customers
Treasury operates a tracing book, however currently there are no exposures as ai the end of 2013 (2012 Eni}

‘The Bank assesses the interest rate nsk arising within the liquid asset portfolios via key measures, Value at Risk and PV01, with assocated delegated limits
These are supplemented by additional controls such as maturity and stop loss mits
3.1.2.1 Value at risk (VaR) Audited

VaR measures the daily maximum potential gam or loss due to market volatlty with a statistical confidence level of 95% and a one-day holding penod
The VaR methodology empioyed is historical senulation using a time series of one year to latest day and was £0 4m at 31 December 2013 for the teasury
portfolos 2012 £0 4m)

A number of imitations should be considered in relation to the VaR model

» historic data is not necessanly a rellable prectctor of future events,

* the model, by definition, does not capture potential losses outside the 95% confidence level, particularly those events that are extreme in nature, and
* VaR 1s based on exposures at close of busmess and, therefore, does not necessanly reflect ntra-day exposures

The outputs from the VaR model were reviewed n 2013 to ensure that they were consistent with methodology employed The Bank cloes not have PRA
VaR model permission and VaR 1 not used m regulatory reporting The maturity method 1s used for reporting general interest rate risk for prudential
Teporting purposes

3.4.2.2 PVO1 Audited

The Bank alsa employs PVO1 which dlustrates the change in valuation on a fixed income portiolo given a 1 basis pont increase in rates A negate PVO1
figure relates to a net asset position and will create a loss if rates rise A positive PVO1 figure relates to a net habilty position and will create a profit if rates nse
The effects of a 1 basis point increase in mlerest rates are £0 Oim (2012 £0 04m) and a 1 basis pont decrease £0 01m (2012 £0 04m)

3.1.3 Swap spread risk Audited

The Bank 1s also exposed to swap spread nsk arising within the liquid asset portiolios whereby a proportion of assets are held in fixed rate government securities
and are accompanied by an interest rate swap to hedge agamst changes in interest rates However, there remains a risk associated with the possible movement
in the spread between sovereign debt yields and swap rates and therefore creates market value volatility For example the market value falls f the yretd on the
sovereign debt ncreases more than swap rates, as the market value loss on the sovereign debt will then be tygher than the market value profit on the swap

The ask ts only crystallised if the sovereign debt and the associated swap are sold The market value movements are monitored and the risk is controlled via
goverment versus swap mark to market limits

3.2 Currency risk Audited

The Bank's treasury foreign exchange actmtes primarily nvolve

© providing a service in meeting the foreign exchange requrements of customers,

* maintaining liquidity m euros and US dollars by rarsing funds and nvesting these to generate a return, and

© performing limited intraday trading and overnight positioning mn major currencies to generate meremental income

4, Operational risk

In plain english:
Operational nsk 1s the risk of logs resultmg from inadequate or fated processes, people and systems within the Bank or from external events

4.1 Operational risk framework

The Bank's approach to the management of operational risk is designed to assist the Bank to safely operate within a Board approved risk appetite, particularly
with regards to protecung the volatility of earnings, reducmg capital requirements, mamntaining the Bank’s reputation as a trusted prowder of financial services
and meeting regulatory expectations. Various elements of the framework were concluded to be ineffective in early 2013 and continues to he strengthened in kne
with good practice and regulatory guidance

Operational risks are identified, managed and mitgated through on-going isk management practices mcluding material risk assessments, risk event reporting,
operational Joss data analysis, a detailed risk control self-assessment process (which 1s bemg refreshed), monitoring of key risk metrics, and scenario analysis
Significant operational nsks are reported to the ORF, the ERC and the BRC These meet regularly to monitor the development and effectiveness of the Risk
Management Framework and the management of significant risks withn the Bank

4.2 Corporate insurance programme

The Bank has a structured insurance programme designed to transfer the impact of specific operational risks in line with the appetite of the organisation and
Industry best practice For example

© msurance of the Bank's buikiings and assets,

© protection of revenue in the event of busmess interruption,

® protection agamst impacts of financial crime,

professional mdemnity and Directors and Officers lability cover; and
© motor, employer, product and public liability insurance

5, Reputational risk

In plain english:

Reputational nek ts the risk that the Bank’s image m the eyes of Its customers, its employees and other businesses it works with could be damaged as a
result of poor decision making, performance, the impact of operational failures or other external issues

As part of the assessment and control of reputational risk, the business performance and risk profile across all risk themes are closely monitored and reviewed
The busmess proactively monitors and manages media, public and customer opinion and works closely with external rating agencies to ensure far and balanced
representation This approach helps maintain investor, customer and market confidence

Reputational ssues include, but are not limited to

© the reputational damage arising from downgrades to the Bank's credit ratings,

© being required to raise further capital n the future, which could affect, or be perceived to alfect, confidence in the Bank,
© fatlure to implement the Bank's strategy.

© areduction in the Bank's customer service levels resultmg from cost-cutung to the Bank's Core business and/or the reonentation of the Bank's
distribution channels,

« the Bank no longer beg owned by The Co-operative Group with the result that the mutual and ethical reputation of The Co-operatwe Group could cantlict
with the profit maxamisation objective of other holders of Ordinary Shares and the Board's duties to such shareholders,

© any potential impact to the Bank's brand or reputation in the event that the shareholding of The Co-operative Group reduces below its current shareholding,

10. Pension risk

In plain english:

Pension risk is the risk that the Bank will be required to pay more than expected into its employees’ pensions schemes in order that the schemes can pay the
pensions to which its employees, former employees and retirees are entitled as they fall due

The Co-operative Group Pension Scheme (Pace) is used by all employees in The Co-operative Group, including the Bank Pace ts a hybrid scheme, consisting
of both a defined benefit and defined contnbution scheme

Fallowang its separation from The Co-operative Group, the Bank is still a participating member of the Pace scheme As a mult-employer pension scheme, Pace
exposes the Bank to risks associated with current and former employees of other Co-operative Group companies Risks are identified at the Co-operative Group
tevel, with the impact of any potential changes to contribution assessed under the Bank's Risk Management Framework

The Bank is also exposed to pension nsk through the Britannia pension scheme In 2009, following the transfer of engagements of Britanrua Building Society,
CFS Management Services Ltd (a subsidiary of The Co-operative Banking Group Ltd) became principal employer of the scheme This scheme closed on
6 October 2010 with active members at the date of closure being invited to join Pace

Pension risk is defined under the Risk Management Framework as the risk to capital and funds from exposure to pension liabilities (to the extent babies are
not met by scheme assets) and nsks mherent mn the valuation of scheme habilites and assets The Bank ts assessing Its pension risk appetite and the appropriate
Teasures to help manage pension nsk The Bank has analysed pension risk into seven sub-categories as follows

* scheme membership — the profile of scheme membership impacis cost and nsk,

* market nsk — the valuation of pension habilties is reliant on the level of government gilt yelds and inflaton Market fluctuations in these factors impact the
value of abilities,

* investment risk ~ asset values are vulnerable to volatle asset markets,

® credit risk — concentration of credit nsk across counterparty, market sector, geography and asset type may increase risk,

* longevity — valuation of pension habihtes is relant on scheme member longevity,

* Jegislavon and regulation ~ pension schemes are highly reguiated and complex, changes :n legislation and regulation may increase cost and risk, and

* strategy and sttucture ~ any changes m Corporate strategy or structure may impact on pension risk and cost

Controls are appled to mitigate these neks and a pension nsk report ss made to the ERC each month to monitor pension nsk and decide if further action is

necessary The report takes account of the nsk reporting delvered to pension scheme trustees The Bank is able to engage directly with the Trustees of the
Bntannia pension scheme and can influence the Trustees of Pace through its relationship with the sponsor, The Co-operative Group

The trustee of Pace 1s primarily responsible for the nsk management arrangements for Pace, agreeing suitable contnbutian rates with the principal employer,
investment strategy and taking professional advice as appropriate The trustee of the Britanma scheme is primarily responsible for the risk management
arrangements for the Britannia scheme, agreeing suitable contribution rates with the principal employer, investment strategy and for taking professional
advice as appropriate

The Pace scheme is not sectionalised and operates on a ast man standing’ basis In the event that other participating employers become insolvent and the
full statutory debt 1s not recovered on msolvency, the Bank would become lable for the rernaining liabilties

The Bank ts therefore exposed to potential future ncreases m required contributans and capital set aside for pension nsk

Separation from The Co-operative Group

Refer to page 48 (Audit Committee report), page 153 (critical judgements and

¢ The risk The completion of the LME on the 20 December 2013

The accounting for the LME was complex and resulted in significant
amounis, including the profit on competion of the LME, being booked n
the financial statements The Directors have also had to exercise significant
Judgement in assessing and measuring the impacts, in particular the
identification of potential obligations (including pension obligations} arising
from the future completion of the separation process This is therefore
considered a key audit risk.

Our response’ Our audit procedures have included, amongst others,
considering whether the accounting for the LME was in accordance

with the relevant accounting standards and interpretations With the
assistance of our valuation specialists we challenged the assumptions
underpinning the valuations of the shares and financial mistruments which

of the profit realised on completion of the LME We compared key inputs

Bank’s 2014-2018 Plan underpinning the Tumnaround Plan We consklered
the separation agreements entered into and related documents and
evaluated the extent to which potential obligations existed and the
appropnateness of the accounting for these, including the assumptions
made and key judgements applied We assessed whether accounting for
the current pension arrangements in 2013 as defined contribution plans
was appropnate and in accordance with accountng standards We also
assessed the adequacy of disclosure concerning additonal tabilities that

of its separation from The Co-operative Group

1. Basis of preparation and significant accounting policies

1.1 Basis of preparation

Both the Company financial statements and the Bank financial statements have been prepared and approved by the Directors in accordance with Intematonat
Financial Reporting Standards (FRS) as issued by the International Accounting Standards Board (ASB), and as adopted by the European Union (EU)

On including the parent company financial statements here together with the Bank financial statements, the Company 1s taking advantage of the exemption in
Section 408 of the Companies Act 2006 not to present its individual mcome statement and related notes that form a part of these approved financial statements

The financial statements have been prepared under the historic cost convention as modified by the revaluation of available for sale financial assets, derivate
contracts, investment properties and certain other financial assets and financial liabiittes held at far value The Bank apphes the recognition, measurement and
disclosure requirements of IFRS in issue that are endorsed by the EU and are effective for accounting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013

The financaal statements compnse all audited sections of the accounts Where indicated, the risk managernent section on pages 69 to 116 and capital
management section on pages 117 to 121 form part of the audited accounts

Standards and interpretations issued and effective

* Amendments to IFRS 7 (Disclosures Offsetting Financial Assets and Liabilities (2011))

arrangements, on the entity's financial position The amendment to IFRS 7 has no maternal impact on the financial statements of the Bank

© IFRS 13 (Far Value Measurement (2011)

additional disclosures set out in note 41 The primary changes in disclosure resulted from the Bank’s additional presentavon of fair value tables analysing levels
of observable inputs

© Amended IAS 1 (Presentation of Items of Other Comprehensive income (2011)
This amendment’s objective is to spht the presentation of other comprehensive mcome mio two sections The different sections will distnguish between
tems which may of may not be recycled mto any future income or expense The arnendment to IAS 1 has no material impact on the financial information
of the Bank

« Revised IAS 19 (Employee Benefits (2011)
This revision means that actuanal gains or losses will now be immediately recognised within income or expense Furthermore, the revision replaces the
‘expected return on assets’ with a net mterest amount calculated by applying the discount rate to the net defined benefit habiityasset The discount rate is
determined by reference to market yields on high quality corporate bonds

Given the Bank continues to account on a defined contribution basis, for what ss now a mult-emplayer pension scheme, the adoption of this revised standard
resulted solely in additional disclosure (see note 36) with no impact to the moome statement

amortsed cost or at far value

The hedge accounting requirements in IFRS 9 align more closely with nek management, resultmg in more uselul miormation io users of financial statements

model in AS 39

© IFRIC 21 (Lewes (2013)
This interpretation gives guidance on the recognition of a lability to pay a fevy that s accounted for in accordance with IAS 37 Provisions, Contingent Liabilities
and Contingent Assets A fevy ts an outflow of resources embodying economic benefits that is imposed by govemments on entlues in accordance with
legisiation other than outflows of resources covered by other standards, fines or other penalties that are imposed for breaches of the regulation The obligating
event that gives rise to a lability to pay a levy is the acimity that triggers the payment af the levy, as identified by the legislation

The Bank will adopt this interpretation from 14 January 2014, when recognising the Fmancial Services Compensation Scheme (FSCS) Levy The impact of the
application will be to increase brought forward reserves at 1 January 2014 by £26 3m as the FSCS Levy will be recognised annually on 1 April, rather than the
date upon which the Bank's share of the levy 1s calculated as a proportion of the total market protected deposit, 31 December

1.3 Going concern

a) Introduction

These financial statements are prepared on a going concem basis The Directors have a reasonable expectation that the Bank will have the resources to conbnue
in business for the foreseeable future, taking into account the matters referred to below

As discussed earler in these accounts, m order to address the capital shortfall identsfied in 2013, the Dwectors defined

« ARecapitaleaton Plan principally compnsing the Leabiity Management Exererse (LME) and the 2014 capital contribution from The Co-operatwe Group
« A Turnaround Plan which involves reshaping and restructurng the busmess as a core relationship bank

Both the remaiing components of the Recapitalisation Plan and the Tumaround Plan are reflected in the 2014-2018 Phan

The assessment of the appropriateness of using the going concern basis of accounting has been subject to a thorough process mvolang analysis and discussion
by management, Executiws and Board Commuttees and the Board, in line with qur governance processes, and discussion with the PRA This analysis included a
particular focus on the 12 month penod ending 30 April 2015 The forecasts included in the 2014-2018 Plan have been subjected to stressed scenarios wich
the Board considered to be reasonable and appropnate

Despite the LME, without which the Directors believe the Bank would have been sulyect to 4 resolution procedure under the Banking Act of 2009, the Bank is
currently below the Individual Capita! Guidance (CG) set by the PRA and it is forecast to remain so for most of the duration of the 2014-2018 planning period

Total Basel It capital resources as at 31 December 2013 are £1 4bn (31 December 2012 £2 3bn) with core tier 1 capital after regulatory deductions of £1 3bn
(31 December 2012 £1 3bn) Following the LME, at 31 December 2013, the Bank's CET1 ratio stands at 7 2% on a Basel Ill end point basis However, this is
only a relatively small margin above the threshold directed by the PRA that, based on Basel tll end pomt rules, mayor UK banks should hold CET1 resources of
at least 7% of their risk-weighted assets by December 2013, and provides limited protection against unexpected losses

The annual results for 2013 reflect significant charges which exceed the levels originally antpated when the Recapitalisation Plan was announced on
4 November 2013 The Directors identified a number of rsks as part of that plan but did not anticipate the extent to which those nsks would materialise
in November, the Bank stated that it ‘expects to report for the end of 2013 a CET1 ratio towards the upper end of previously announced guidance, on
29 August 2013, of below 9% but above the regulatory mmimum requirement’ The CET1 rato of 7 2% at 31 December 2013 ts above the requirement
but lower than expected and, without additional CET1, would be projected to fail below 7% durmg the penod of the 2014-2018 Plan

Consequently, as announced on 24 March 2014, additional CET1 needs to be rased m order to deliver the Tumaround Plan The Directors have put steps in
place to raise ths additional requirement Please see page 22 for the latest update in this regard The PRA has reviewed and accepted the 2014-2018 Plan,
including the further capital raising discussed above

f) Conclusion

The Directors have concluded that whilst the competion of the LME puts the Bank in a stronger position, its capital position has been weakened by the
crystallisation of certain risks, including conduct and legal risks and the costs of separation, in the 2013 results This situation, along with the risks set out
above, and ther consequential effects, represent a material uncertainty which may cast doubt upon the Bank's ability to continue as a going concem The Bank
may, therefore, be unable to continue realising its assets and discharging sts habiities in the normal course of business Nevertheless, after making enquires
and considering the current forecasts, in particular those for the period up to and including 30 April 2015, the Directors have a reasonable expectation that the
Bank will have adequate resources to continue tn business over this period For these reasons, they continue to adopt the gomg concern basis in preparing these
financial statements This set of financial statements does not include the adjustments that would result if the Bank was unable to continue as a going concem

1.4 Significant accounting policies

The accounting policies set out below have been applied consistently to all periods, inclusive of assets recognised where the Bank ts subject to the substantial
risks and rewards of those assets

1. Business combinations

2. Basis of consolidation

a) Subsidiaries

Subsidiaries are entities controlled by the Company Control exists whenever the Company has the power to gover the financial and operating policies of an
entity so as to obtam benefits from its actwities In assessing control, potential voting rights that presently are exercisable are taken into account The fnancral
information of subsidiaries is included in the consolidated financial information from the date that control commences until the date that control ceases

The financial information has been prepared using uniform accounting policies and is based on the same accounting period as the Company

Intra-group balances and transactions, and any unrealised gains and losses arising from intra-group transactions, are elyminated in preparing the consoldated
financial information

Special purpose entities (SPEs) are entities that are created to accomplish a narrow and well defined objective, for the Bank this cludes
* various securitisation transactions in which mortgages were sold to SPEs The equity of these SPEs is not owned by the Company, and

* Covered Bond Limited Liability Partnerships created in order to act as a guaranior for the issue of covered bonds (refer to note 41 on page 218 ior
further information}

An SPE ts consolidated if, based on an evaluation of the substance of its relationship with the Bank and the SPE’s risks and rewards, the Company concludes
that it controls the SPE

The following circumstances may indicate a relationship in which, in substance, the Company controls and consequently consolidates an SPE

© the activites of the SPE are being conducted on behalf of the Company according to its specific business needs so that the Bank obtams benefits from the
SPE's operation,

© the Company has the decision making powers ito obtain the majonty of the benefits of the actimties of the SPE,

© the Company has the rights to obtam the majority of the benefits of the SPE and therefore may be exposed to the nsks mcidental to the actmities of the SPE, or
© the Company retams the majority of the residual or ownership risks related to the SPE or its assets in order to obtain benefits from tts actmties

The above circumstances apply to all Company SPEs Consequently the Company consolidates each SPE

The assessment of whether the Company has control over an SPE 1s cared out at inception No further assessment of control is carried out unless changes in
the structure or terms of the SPE or additional transactions between the Bank and the SPE occur

3, Revenue recognition

a) Interest income and expense

Interest income and expense is recognised on an effective interest rate (EIR) basis, mclustve of directly attnbutable incremental transaction costs and fees
including arrangement and broker tees, valuation and solicitor costs, discounts and premmums where appropriate and early redemption fees.

The EIR basis spreads the interest income and expense over the expected life of each mstrument The EIR is the rate that, at the mception of the instrument,
exactly discounts expected future cash payments and receipts through the expected Ife of the instrument back to the invtal carrying amount When calculating
the EIR, the Bank estimates cash flows considering all contractual terms of the instrument (for example, prepayment options) but does not consxter assets’

future credit losses except for assets acquired at a deep discount

For assets acquired at a value significantly below the carrying value in the acquiree’s fmancial nformatton because they have incurred loss, expectations of future
loss are higher than at origination, and interest spreads have widened because of deteriorating market conditions, the calculation of EIR ts the same as shown
above with the exception that the estimates of future cash flows include credit losses

b) Fees and commissions

Fee and commission ncome ts predominanily made up of arrangement and other fees relating to loans and advances to customers that are mcluded in the
effective interest calculation Commitment fees recewed are deferred and included tn the EIR calculation upon completion or taken in full at the date the
commitment period expires and completion does not occur

Ail other fee and commission income, such as loan closure fees or arrears fees, not included in the effectrve interest calculation, ts recognised on an accruals
basis as the service is provided

Fees and commissions payable to introducers in respect of obtaining lending business, where these are direct and incremental costs related to the issue of a
financial instrument, are included in interest income as part of the effective interest rate

a} Recognition
The Bank initally recognises loans and advances when they are advanced to customers Deposits, debt secunties issued and other borrowed funds are
recognised on the date at which they are originated

Regular way purchases and sales of financial assets are recognised on the trade date at which the Bank commits to purchase or sell the asset. All other financial
assets and liabilities are initally recognised on the trade date at which the Bank becomes a party to the contractual provisions of the instrument I

b) Derecognition
The Bank derecagnises a financial asset when the contractual rights to the cash flows from the financial assets expe, or it transfers the nghts to receive the

contractual cash flows m a transacten nm which substantally all of the nsks and rewards of ownership of the financial asset are transferred or in which the Bank
neither transfers nor retains substantially all of the risks and rewards of ownership and it does not retam Control of the financial asset

When a financial asset is derecognised mn its entirety, the difference between the carrying amount and the sum of the consideration recered (including any new
asset obtamed less any new hatuity assumed), and any curnulative gan or loss that had been recognised wn other comprehensive ncome, ts recognised in the
income statement

When available for sale financial assets are derecognised, the cumulative gain or lass, mcluding that previously recognised in reserves, is recognised in the
Income slatement

derecognised and the consideration pard is recognised through the income statement.

expected to be ‘highly effective’ on offsetting the changes in fair value or cash flows of the respective hedged items during the period for which the hedge is

| Cashflow hedges

Where dervatnes are designated as hedges of the exposure to variability in cash flows of a recognised asset or lability, or a highly probable forecast transaction,

gan or loss is recognised in the mcome statement immediately

of expense When a hedging msirument expires of 1s sold, or when a hedge no fonger meets the criteria for hedge accounting, any cumulative gain or loss
recognised at that ime remams m equity until the forecast transaction is eventually recognised m the mcome statement

When a forecast transaction 1s no longer expected to occur, the cumulative gain or loss that was recognised in equity is immediately reclassified to the
Income statement

12. Goodwill

and joint ventures and represents the difference between the cost of the acquisition and the fair value of the identifiable assets, liabilities and contingent

tf a business combmation ts achieved without transfer of consideration, the amount of goodwill is calculated by reference to the far value of the Bank's interest
in the acquiree using a valuation technique The technique involves assessing the future net cash flow of the acquiree and then discounting using a rate that
reflects current market assessment of the ume value of money and risks specific to the acquiree

Goodwill is stated at cost less any accumulated impaurment losses Goodwill is not amortised but 1s tested for impairment on an annual basis Where impairment
ts required, the amount ts recognised in the ncome statement and cannot be subsequently reversed

16, Income tax
a) Overview

Tax for the year comprises current and deferred tax, which is recognised in the come statement except to the extent that it relates to tems recognised directly
in equaty, in which case it 1s recognised in the statement of comprehensive Income

b) Current tax

Current tax is the expected tax payable on the taxable income for the year, using tax rates enacted or substantively enacted at the balance sheet date, and any
adjustment to tax payable in respect of previous years

c) Deferred tax

Deferred tax is proved usmg the balance sheet method, providing for temporary differences between the carrying amounts of assets and habilties for financial
reporting purposes and the amounts used for taxation purposes The amount of deferred tax provided for ts based on the expected manner of realisation or
settlement of the carrying amount of assets and habiltves, using tax rates enacted or substantwvely enacted at the balance sheet date

A deferred tax asset 1s recognised only to the extent that its probable that future taxable profits will be available agamnst which the asset can be ublsed and s
supported by the Board's approved five year plan

Deferred tax assets are reviewed at each reportmg date and are reduced to the extent that t rs no tonger probable that the related tax benefit will be realised

)) Retail collectve

The provision rates reftect the hkelihood that the debt in that category/age wil be written off or charged off at some point n the future The rates are based

f. Pensions

i. Defined contribution accounting for the Pace scheme

The Bank participates in The Co-operative Pension Scheme (Pace) Pace ts a hybrid scheme, consisting of a defined benefit section and a defined
contribution section

At31 December 2012 the scheme was a Group plan, since all participating entities were within The Co-operative Group It was accounted for on a defined
contribution basis smce there was no contractual agreement or stated Co-operative Group policy for charging the net defined benefit cost for the scheme as a
whole to individual Co-operative Group entities Therefore, the Bank did not recognise its share of the net defined benefit cost The net defined benefit cost of the
pension scheme was recognised fully by the sponsoring employer, which is The Co-operative Group Limited

On 4 November 2013 The Co-operative Group and the Bank entered into an undertaking pursuant to which The Co-operative Group agreed with the Bank,
subject to certain exceptions, not to require the Bank to cease to participate in Pace in connection with the Liability Management Exercise or any subsequent
reduction in The Co-operative Group's shareholding in the Bank (including to nil) Should ether The Co-operatrve Group or the Bank so request, the parties will
enter into good faith discussions to agree on the separation of Pace, so that the scheme lability property attributable to the Bank and an equivalent proportion of
the scheme’s assets would be transferred to a separate tax registered pension scheme

Neither party shall be under an obligation to agree to any separation of the scheme that would result in a requirement to make matenal payments to or mn respect
of the scheme

Following separation of the Bank from the wider Co-operative Group as a result of the Liability Management Exercise, the Bank \s still a participating employer in
the Pace scheme and the Pace scheme ts considered to be a mutti-erployer scheme under [AS 19 Employee benefits (fevsed 2011)

As a mult-employer pension scheme, Pace exposes the participating businesses to actuarial risks associated with the current and former employees of the other
Co-operative Group companies The proportion of Pace liabilities accrued by members whilst employees of Bank are believed to represent a minonty of total
Pace liabilities Bank could however be lable for funding a greater proportion of Pace labikties

There are for example liabilities m Pace relating to benefits accrued by members whilst employed by CFSMS and working in the Bank's business On 23 January
2014, following the legal separation of the Bank from the wider Co-operative Group, employment contracts for those employees who spend the majority of

thei time working on behalf of the Bank were transferred from CFSMS to the Bank This will ncrease the number of Bank employees participating in the Pace
scheme in 2014

to agree any separation of the Pace scheme that would result in material payments to the scheme However, The Co-operative Group has undertaken to agree with
the Bank sts proportion of employer contributions n Pace and, if not agreed, the matter wil be referred to an independent third party Accordingly, there is a wide
Tange of outcomes regarding the duration and contnbution requirements of a new schedule of contnbutions which make the overall lability uncertain

To the best of the Directors’ knowledge and belief, having made reasonable enquires, there Is no contractual agreement determining how the deficit recovery
contributions are funded by the participants, the Bank has not recognised a liability in respect of any part of the existing deficit funding

A formal irennal valuation of Pace as at 5 April 2013 6 underway but has not yet been completed When completed it may reveal a significant funding deftctt
There ig therefore a risk that in future periods the Bank will recognise significant kabiities in respect of the scheme in its accounts

g. Liability Management Exercise (LIME)

The Bank carried out a Liabiity Management Exercise (LME) on 20 December 2013 Details of the LME can be found in note 4 It has been accounted for in
accordance with IFRIC 19 (Extinguishing Financial Liabilities wih Equity Instruments)

The existing ordinary share capital of the Bank was extinguished The then perpetual and dated subordinated debt were extinguished and replaced with new
dated subordinated bonds and ordinary shares The preference shares were transferred to The Co-operative Group (wa The Co-operative Banking Group) and
waned all nghts to mcome and capital

‘The difference between the carrying amount of the extinguished debt and fair value of the consideration (the fair value of new dated subordinated debt and
ordinary shares ssued) has been recognised m the income statement In accordance with company law, which requires that share premium is determined by
reference to the value of debt extnguished, this income was subsequently transferred from retamed earings to the share premium account

The key judgement in accounting for the LME relates to the valuation of the Bank, which directly determines the value of new shares issued The Bank was valued
at £790m ging nse to a £688m credit to the income statement. A 10% change mn the valuation would gre nse fo a £79m change m the mcome statement.

The valuation 1s highly judgemental and in order to determine an appropriate figure, management calculated a market valuation in line with IFAS 13 Fair Value
Measurement), discounted to take account af market imperfections The key assumptions mn the valuation were the market value of the exsting bonds
extinguished and the 20% management applied discount for market imperfections due to a relabvely thn volume of bond transactions

Ths market valuation was compared to a set of itemaily generated valuation models using the Bank's plan These models were subjective and were based
on assumptions including drscount rate and the number of years used mn the valuation These generated a range of valuations of £674m to £1 2bn, depending
on the assumptions used The discounted value of £790m sat at the lower end of ths range

The overall capital position of the Bank 1s unaffected by a change in the valuation of the Bank as any change in the credit to the come statement is offset
by an equal and opposite change m the value of new shares

3. Restatement

In plain english:

This note prowdes details of a restatement of the 2012 balance sheet values for tangible, mtangible and mtercompany assets and the associated impact to
the 2012 income statement and statement of cash flows

Histoncally the intangible asset for the core IT banking system replacement had been recorded in the books of CFS Management Sermces Ltd (CFSMS),

(@ service company which was a fellow Co-operative Banking Group subsidiary), with appropriate recharge to the Bank wa intercompany balances payable
The development of the core IT bankung system was {unded by the Bank. CFSMS had iimited other assets with which to absorb any potential losses that may
have ansen due to difficulties in development

The Directors have revewed and reconsidered the accounting treatment of the intangible asset m development and all other assets held on the balance sheet of
CFSMS which were used solely by the Bank. The Directors have concluded that the Bank was substantially exposed to the nsks and rewards of these assets and
after considering the funding of the asset and CFSMS's lack of assets to absorb losses, the appropriate accounting treatment would be to hold these assets on
the balance sheet of the Bank This has been reflected in the Bank and Company Annual Report and Accounts

4. Recapitalisation and the Liability Management Exercise

tn plain english:

This note provdes details of the Liatabty Management Exercise which was completed in December 2013 and the significant impact it had on the Bank's
capital and reserve position

Dunng the year the Bank cared out a significant refinancing exercise with the aim of increasing the level of capital held This exercise involved issuing
New shares and new debt in exchange for cash and extinguishing existing debt and preference shares The background to this is discussed in the Capital
Management section

The Bank announced, in November 2013, a Recapitalisation Plan to generate £1 5bn additional CET1, which was discussed with the relevant regulatory bodies
The key objective of the Plan was to stengthen significantly the Bank's CET1 base wath the support and participation of certain holders of the Bank's securities
and without the need for support from the tax payer

The £1 5bn Recapitalisation Plan consisted of three elements

a) The Labsity Management Exercise (LME) to generate c £1 2bn of new CETi,

b) The commitment of The Co-operative Group to contnbute £333m of CET1 by the end of 2014, and

©) £40m of CET1 generated in 2014 from mterest savings on the securities surrendered in the LME

The transaction has been accounted for in accordance wath IFRIC 19 Extinguishing Fmancial Liabilities with Equity Instruments

Details of the Liability Management Exercise

a) The LME
A core part of the Recaprtalsation Plan was achieved through successful completion of the LME on 20 December 2013 This mvolved the following elements

1 The wssyance of £0 2bn of new subordinated debt and £0 6bn of new ordinary shares in exchange for £1 3bn of exsting debt and preference shares and
additional contnbutons from bondholders of £0 1ba,

1 The cancellation of £0 4bn of exsting ordinary share capital, which resulted mn the creation of a capital redemption reserve of the same value, and

i The sate of investment assets that were used as economic hedges for the existing debt, generating a profit of £11 1m These assets had been accounted
for a5 avattable for sale, and therefore there was an equivalent reduction m the available tor sale reserve

b) The contribution of £333m by The Co-operative Banking Group. This is due or paid up as fallaws:

1 £20m paid in December 2013,

n €50m pad in January 2014,

i £100m due by 30 June 2014, and

W £163m due by 31 December 2014

¢) The balance of the £1.5bn is £40m of savings in 2014 generated on securities surrendered as part of the LME
Elements a) and b) together created additonal CET1 m the following ways

| New ordinary shares ~— a total of £0 8bn of new ordinary shares were sssued to both existing bondholders and The Co-operative Banking Group This was
recorded as share capital and share premmum as shown in the table below

i Income — £0 7bn was recognised in the ncome statement This arose because the amount of debt extnguished and contnbutions recened (£1 3bn and
£0 4bn) was greater than the amount of the new debt and shares ssued (20 2bn and £0 8bn) In accordance with company law, which requires that share
premium ts determined by reference to the amount of debt extnguished, the element of mcome relatmg to new shares issued (£0 6bn) was transferred from

There are a number of reasons for the merease in the overall impasment charge Firstly, m the first half of 2013, the amount of assets designated as Non-core

and resulting in mcreased imparments This took to account the continuing impacts of the corporate real estate environment, prolonged real wage deflation

changes in customer circumstances have also been incurred in both the Core and Non-core businesses.

levels to the first half, offset by reductions in corporate collective provisions as the book continues to be worked out and de-risked and reductions in Optimum
and Reta provisions due to improvements in residential property collateral values from strong house price growth

19. Investment securities

In plain english:

We hold a number of different investments in the form of securites (ie listed & unlisted shares and bonds} We are obliged to categorise and value these
as follows

Loans and receivables These are included in the balance sheet at amortrsed cost Jess any imparments

* Available for sale These are investments that are not included in another category (above or below) They are revalued at each balance sheet date (marked
to market) Any increases in value are taken directly to equity, so do not appear in the income statement (see ihe statement of comprehensive income)

* Fair value through income or expense These are nvestments that we hold short term They are revalued at each balance sheet date (marked to market)
Any increases in value are profits and reductions in value are losses, both of which are recorded in the income statement

« Held for trading These were our trading assets, and were revalued daily (mark to market) We no longer hald mvestment securities for trading purposes

20. Derivative financial instruments

In plain engtish:

A denvative 6 a financial instrument used to manage risk Its value changes over ime mn response to underlying vanables such as mterest rates or exchange
tates These contracts are for a fixed period Our derivatives inchide a vanety of financial contracts, many of which are designed to help customers manage
thew interest rate and currency nsks We also use derivatives ourselves, to mariage our own mterest rate and foreign currency nek

The value of the derwatwe contracts that you can see m our balance sheet is determined by fluctuations m the underlyng item The dervative will appear as
an asset if we have benefitted from the contract by the balance sheet date and as a hability if we have not benefitted

AS you can imagine, the value of these derivatives 1s very volatile as market conditions change on a daily basis

The Bank has entered, as principal, mto various derivatives either as a trading actwity, which includes proprietary transactions and customer faciitation, or as a
hedging actmty for the management of interest rate risk, equity nsk and foreign exchange rate nsk Positive and negative far values have not been netted as the
Bank does not have a legal right of offset

Derivatives held for trading purposes

The trading transactions are wholly interest rate related contracts including swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures
Trading transactions include derivatives where the Bank enters into a transaction to accommodate a customer together with the corresponding hedge
transaction The Bank no longer holds derivatives tor trading purposes

Non-trading derivatives

Non-trading transactions comprise derivatives held for hedging purposes to manage the asset and liability positions of the Bank. Derivatives used to manage
interest rate related positions include swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures The foreign exchange rate positions are
Managed using forward currency transactions and swaps Equity risk ts managed using equity swaps

Dunng the year the Bank has entered into fair value hedges to mitigate price movements due to interest rate sensitivities

Property

The Bank has a number of leasehold properties available for rent Provisons are made when either the sub-lease income does not cover the rental expense

or the property 1s vacant The provision 1s based on the expected outflows during the remaming periods of the leases using a discount rate of 3.5% ln addition,
dilapidation provisions are recorded to the extent that the Bank has mourred dilapidations and/or the dilapdation clause within the contract has been invoked
Ounng the year £11 5m (2012 £m} has been provided for this

Financial Services Compensation Scheme (FSCS) levies

The FSCS has provided compensation to customers of financial mstitutions following the collapse of deposit takers in 2008 The compensation pard out to
consumers ts currently funded through loans from HM Treasury The Bank will be lable to pay a proportion of the outstanding borrowings that the FSCS has
borrowed from HM Treasury Additionally, the Bank 1s obliged to pay its share of management expenses and compensation based upon the Bank's proportion
of the total market protected deposits at 31 December of each year

The ultimate FSCS levy to the mdustry as a result of the 2008 collapses cannot currently be estimated reliably as it is dependent on other factors that may
affect amounts payable and the tming of amounts payable, including changes m mterest rates, potential recoveries of assets by the FSCS and the level of
protected deposits

The Bank has provided £39 6m (2012 £38 6m) for its share of the levies raised by the FSCS mcluding the interest on the loan from HM Treasury in respect

of the levy years to 31 March 2015 The provision includes £27 5m in respect of the mterest fevy (2012 £277m) The Bank's interest levy provision calculation
includes estimates of the total FSCS levy in each levy year and estimates of the Bank’s market participation in each levy year During 2012, the FSCS indicated
that it expected to raise a capital tevy to cover the estimated shortfall in the amounts recovered from the fatled banks to repay HM Treasury loans made to the
FSCS The Bank has provided £12 1m (2012 £10 9m) in respect of rts share of this levy

Payment Protection Insurance (PPI)

Prowsions have been made in respect of potential customer compensation claims relating to past sales of PPI Claims are investigated on an individual basis and,
where appropriate, compensation payments are made For a number of years, the Bank, along with many other financial services providers, sold PP! alongside
mortgage and non-mortgage credit products The Bank stopped selling non-morigage PP! in January 2009 and stopped selling mortgage PPI in March 2012

The FSA issued a policy statement in August 2010, which amended the ‘Disputes Resolution Complamts' section of the FSA Handbook, setting out new

rules for handling complants, including complamts of PPI mis-selling The Bank must comply with the policy statement which requires complainants to

receive adequate redress and the Bank to deliver far outcomes and treat customers fairly including non-complainants An additional provision of £103 Om
(2012 £149 7m) has been recognised in the year (note 9), n respect of the total expected cost to the Bank of carrying out this work and paying compensation,
making total provisions raised of £3470m (2012 £244 Om) Refer to note 2 for further deta! on PPI

36, Pensions

In plain english:

The Co-operative Group Pension Scheme (Pace) ts used by all companies m The Co-operative Group and in which the Bank continues to participate Pace is
ahyonid scheme, consisting of a defined benefit section and a defined contribution section

Ordinarily, defined benefit schemes appear as an asset (when in surplus) or as a habibty (wher m deficit} on the balance sheet However, because there is
fo relable basts for mentfying the Bank's share of the assets, labiites and costs we report the scheme as though it were a defined contribution scheme
(money purchase) As a result, the pension scheme does not appear on the balance sheet instead, we just show the pension contnbutions as an expense in
the income statement

This note gives you detailed information on the scheme assets, labilrkes and all the assumptions we have made in reaching these values in addition it
provides details of the Bntannia pension scheme, in which three of the Bank's subsidiaries participate and the Bank's small unfunded scheme

Pension charge

The pension charge in the income statement at 31 December 2013 was £29 3m (2012 £34 Om) which includes £8m (2012 £10m) as the Bank’s contribution
to the Pace deficit recovery charge Of ths, £0 6m (2012 £1 2m) 1s included in operating costs incurred outside the normal course of business

The Bank was charged £277 8m (2012 £291 3m) of employee related costs by CFSMS, of which approximately £24 8m (2012 £20 1m) was im relation to
payments to Pace and £1 0m (2012 £12 3m) was in relation to the Britannia scheme

The Co-operative Pension Scheme (Pace)
The Bank participates in Pace, a hybrid scheme, consisting of a defined benefit secton and a defined contribution section

At31 December 2012 the scheme was a group plan, smce afi participating entities were within The Co-operative Group jt was accounted for on a defined

Consumer Credit Act issues

The Consumer Credit Act regulates consumer lending and governs the way in which entities, including banks, providng consumer credit to retall customers
carry out business From 1 April 2014 the Financial Services and Markets Act 2000 also apples alongside certan retained provisions of the Consumer Credit
Act The Consumer Credit Act includes very detailed, prescnptive and highly technical requirements for lenders affecting customer documentation and which, in
tum, impact how operational processes and IT systems are configured While the Bank bas identified certain mstances where its documentation or processes
have not been fully comphant with the technical requirements and has provided accordingly, there may be other instances which have not yet been identiied
Breaches may have the effect of Inggerng periods of non-compliance dunng which an affected customer is not hable to pay mterest '

Interest refunds would therefore need to be mace in certain cases where a period of non-compliance has been previousty triggered The Bank ts considenng the
operational mplementaton of the refunding of interest to customers where applicable The Bank has not yet determined all aspects of such processes The Bank
has made no provision to pay mierest on the amount of any refund

An amount of £109 5m (2012 £ni) has been recognised to refund mterest following identification of a number of breaches of the requirements of the
Consumer Credit Act As part of this process of identification, detailed and technical legal analysts has been camed out as to whether breaches of the technical
requirements have m fact occurred, the nature of those breaches and the consequences which follow (which differ according to the precise nature of the
breach) Such legal analysis by its nature invoives judgement and assessment of the facts of particular crcumstances In the event that such legal analysis and
judgements are determined to be wrong, the Bank could be exposed to a matenal additional labilty The amount of £109 5m which has been provided is the
best estate of the lability based on the legal analysis

Tax treatment of separation

Until separation of the Bank from The Co-operative Group ts complete, and the new arrangements referred to in the CFSMS-Bank Framework Agreement as
detailed in note 39 have been entered into, the Bank will continue to be responsible for indemnifying CFSMS under the CFSMS-Bank Services Agreement It is
anticipated that the Bank will become the owner of the assets currently held by CFSMS for the provision of services exclusively to the Bank — tt 1s expected that
this will be achieved in a VAT efficient manner, but if this cannot be done, there 1s. a potential VAT cost of £26m on the transfer of the Bank exclusive assets to
the Bank.

As detailed in note 3 to the Bank financial statements, the Directors have reviewed and reconsidered the accounting treatment of the intangible asset in
development and all other assets held on the balance sheet of CFSMS which were used solely by the Bank The Directors have concluded that the Bank was
substantially exposed to the nsks and rewards of these assets and after considering the fundng of the asset and CFSMG's lack of assets to absorb losses, the
appropriate accounting treatment would be to hold these assets on the balance sheet of the Bank. The Bank has applied an approach to the tax and accounting
treatment of the Bank exclusive assets However, if, and that to the extent that, there is a change to this treatment, there may be an additional tax charge

There will continue to be VAT charges incurred m respect of any assets that are supplied to the Bank under the CFSMS-Bank Sermices Agreement or the
CFSMS-Bank Framework Agreement (as applicable) As such, the Bank will look to mitigate these VAT charges through the transfer of alt or some of such
assets to the Bank as soon as possible

Separation

Prior to the LME, certain functions of the Bank were centralsed and carned out by The Co-operative Group There are two key arrangements m place under
which the Bank receives the supply of services, assets and/or personnel

1 Between 2011 and 2013, the Bank transferred a number of functions and a substantial number of personnel to The Co-operatve Group and entered into
arrangements whereby The Co-operative Group would provide certam services to the Bank. This project is known as Project Unity Two key agreements in
connection with Project Unity are

— the existing 1T Services Agreement in relation to the provision of IT services to the Bank and other members of The Co-operatwe Banking Group, and

— a Professional Master Services Agreement where the same parties entered into a number of individual service contracts in relation to the provision of
other services

2 CFSMS was established as a direct subsidiary of The Co-operative Banking Group m 2005 to enable economies of scale through the shanng of employees
and sourcing of third party services across The Co-operative Bantung Group, mcluding the Bank CFSMS provdes seruces to the Bank under the CFSMS
~~ Bank Services Agreement referred to above, under which the Bank gives a broad indemnity to CFSMS in respect of actmties carried out by CFSMS for
the Bank

Following the announcement of the Bank's Recapitalisation Plan in June 2013 (the June Plan), the Bank and The Co-operative Group worked to amend
certain provisions of the Project Unity and CFSMS Arrangements Those amendments were close to finatisation when The Co-operative Group announced on
21 October 2013 that it was in discussion with bondholders, and that many elements of the June Pian would need to change in material respects to reflect the
fact that the Bank would no longer be a subsidiary of The Co-operative Group The change to the June Pian meant that there would be significant cost impacts
if the Project Unity and CFSMS Arrangements were to continue in the form ongmally envisaged In addition, the Bank did not consider it appropriate for the
provision of personnel or supply of certain thied party services and assets to the Bark to be housed in an entity that has ceased to be part of the same group
as the Bank

Separation Principles

Accordingly the Bank and The Co-operative Group have been working towards renegotiation of the arrangements under which the Bank receives staff and
services from The Co-operative Group These negotiations are complex and ongoing Pending finaksation of the revsed arrangements, the Bank and
The Co-operative Group agreed high level prmaples (the Separation Principles) to govern their separation discussions

The Separation Principles mclude an overarching agreement to act in good faith and, amongst other things

~ until alternative arrangements for Project Unity and the CFSMS Arrangements are agreed, The Co-operative Group will continue to provide services to the
Bank under the same terms that such services are currently provided,

~ for those costs allocated by The Co-operative Group in its sole discretion, the Bank and The Co-operative Group will take reasonable steps to avoid the Bank
INCUNTINg costs Incremental to those it currently incurs,

~~ The Co-operatwe Group and the Bank will mitigate costs arssing for The Co-operatwe Group or the Bank from ()) The Co-operative Group being a less than
50% shareholder of the Bank or (1) agreed steps taken m connection with the separation process,

~ an additional mark-up (of no more than 20%) may be charged by The Co-operative Group for services provided under Project Unity after the LME but not in
respect of the first 12 months in respect of all services,

— costs wil be allocated between the Bank and The Co-operatwe Group on an equitable basis, and

~ a working group consisting of representatives from the Bank and The Co-operative Group wail be established to address and oversee the separation
work-stream

The Co-operative Group and the Bank have also agreed the basis on which Project Unity and the CFSMS Arrangements will be unwound

Project Unity
As part of separation, the Bank will enter into a new IT Services Agreement {the New IT Service Agreement) and a new Master Services Agreement (the
New MSA)

Its intended that the day to day operational management of the services supplied under the New IT Service Agreement is monitored by a Co-operative Group

contract manager {as the supplier of services), a Bank contract manager {as a customer), function leads for The Co-operative Group and the Bank and risk,

audit and comphance for the Bank Under the New IT Service Agreement, The Co-operative Group will provide the Bank with the following services colleague 1
technology services, network services, core services, Service management services and change management services

The New MSA will be a framework agreement intended to establish a flexible contractual arrangement to enable the Bank and CFSMS to obtain certain
professional services from The Co-operative Group as agreed from time to time m service contracts Service contacts will be entered into in relation to
secretariat, legal, corporate affarrs, marketing, finance, corporate HR, people services, estates, Itus (property management), risk and intemal audit
CFSMS Arrangements

41. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities contnued
a) Use of financial instruments

The use of financial mstruments is essential to the Bank's business activites, and financial nstruments constitute a significant proportion of the Bank s assets
and habilittes The main financial instruments used by the Bank, and the purposes for which they are held, are outlined below
Loans and advances to customers and customer accounts

The provision of banking facilities to customers is the prime activity of the Bank, and loans and advances to customers and customer accounts are major
constituents of the balance sheet Loans and advances to customers include retail mortgages, corporate Joans, credit cards, unsecured retail lending and
overdrafts Customer accounts include retail and corporate current and savings accounis

Loans and advances to banks and investment securities

Loans and advances to banks and mvestment secuntes underpin the Bank's lkquxdity requirements and generate incremental net interest and trading income
Held for trading investments are traded solely for short term profit, however the Bank has now ceased this type of trade

Deposits by banks and debt secunties in issue

The Bank issues medium term notes within an established euro medium term note programme and also issues cerbiicates of deposit and commercial paper
as part of Its normal treasury actmities These sources of funds, alongside other borrowed funds, are mvested in marketable investment grade debt secunties
and short tern wholesale market placements and are used to fund customer foans

Other borrowed funds

The Bank utilises a broad spread of long term wholesale funding in the form of fixed rate subordinated debt m addition to funding from ordinary share capital
and retained eammgs Refer to note 31 for details of changes to other borrowed funds as a result of the LME

Derivatives

A derwative ss a financial mstrument that derives its value from an underlying rate or price such as interest rates, exchange rates and other market prices
Derivatives are an efficient means of managing market risk and limiting counterparty exposure The Bank uses them mainly for hedging purposes and to meet
the needs of customers

The most frequently used derivative contracts are interest rate swaps, exchange traded futures and options, caps and floors, currency swaps and forward
currency transactions Terms and conditions are determined by using standard industry documentation Derivatives are subyect to the same market and credit
risk control procedures as are applied to other wholesale market instruments and are aggregated with other exposures to monitor total counterparty exposure
which ts managed within approved kmits for each counterparty

Foreign exchange

The Bank undertakes foreign exchange dealing to faciktate customer requirements and to generate incremental mcome from short term trading in the major
currencies Structured nsk and trading related risk are managed formally wathin position limits which are set by the Assets and |iabilites Committee, to which
authority ts detegated by the Board

41, Fair values of financial assets and liabilities contnued

The carrying values of financial assets and habilties measured at far value are determined in comphance with the accounting policies in note 1 and according
to the following hnerarchy

Level 1 - Quoted market prices in active markets
Financal mstruments with quoted pnces for identical nstrumenis active markets The best evdence of far value ss a quoted market price #1 an actively
traded market

Level 2 ~ Valuation techniques using observable inputs

Financial instruments with quoted prices for similar instruments in active markets or quoted prices for dentical or similar nstruments in mactive markets and
financial instruments valued using models where all significant nputs are observable

The valuation techniques used to value these mstruments empky onty observable market data and relate to the following assets and kabilities

Loans and advances to customers

Loans and advances to customers include corporate loans of £125 5m (2012 £142 4m) which are fair valued through mcome or expense using observable
inputs Loans held at fair value are valued at the sum of all future expected cash flows, discounted using a yreld curve based on observable market mputs

Investment secunties — available for sale

Farr value ts based on available market prices Where this information ts not available, fair value has been estimated using quoted market prices for secunties
with similas credit, maturity and yield characteristics

Derivative financial instruments

Over-the-counter (ie non-exchange traded) derivatives are valued using valuation models which are based on observable market data Valuation models calculate
the present value of expected future cash flows, based upon ‘no arbitrage’ principles The Bank enters into vanilla foreign exchange and mterest rate swap
derivatives, for which modelling techniques are standard across the industry Examples of mputs that are generally observable mchude foreign exchange spot

and forward rates, and benchmark interest rate curves

Investment properties
Investment properties are carried at fair value For those within evel 2, fair value is calculated by using recent valuations of indivdual assets within the portfolio,
indexed linked to the balance sheet date using the relevant regional house price index where appropriate

Customer accounts - capital bonds

The estimated fair value of customer accounts — capital bonds is based on independent third party valuations using forecast future movements in the
appropriate indices

Equity shares

Equity shares pnmanty relate to investments held m Vocalink Limited which are unquoted shares The valuation of these shares 1s based on the Bank's
percentage shareholding and the net asset value of the company according to its most recently published financial statements

Level 3 ~ Valuation techniques using unobservable inputs
‘Ths ts used for financial instruments valued using models where one or more significant inputs are not observable
The small proportion of financial assets valued based on significant unobservable puts are analysed as tollows

Loans and advances to customers

Loans and advances to customers include 25 year fixed rate mortgages of £8 7m (2012 £11 2m) which are fair valued through income or expense using
unobservable inputs 25 year fixed rate mortgages are valued using future mierest cash flows at the fixed customer rate and estimated schedule of customer
repayments Cash flows are discounted at a credit adjusted discount rate, the credit adjustment ts based on the average margin of new long dated (five years
or greater) fixed rate business wnitten m the last sx months, and subject to quarterly review The eventual ummg of future cash flows may be different from that
forecast due to unpredictable customer behaviour, particularly on a 25 year product The valuation methodology takes account of credit nsk and has increased
the valuation by £0 8m in 2013 (2012 £0 Sm increase) A reasonable change in the assumptions would not result n any matenal change in the valuation

41. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities continued

Derivative financial instruments
Dervative financial mstruments in the form of interest rate swaps have been entered wto between the Bank and its subsidiaries, and external counterparties

The purpose of the swaps are to convert the fixed and base rate inked revenue receipts of the poo! of mortgage assets to the same LIBOR inked basis as the
intercompany joan Under this swap arrangement the Bank's subsidiaries pay to the swap counterparty, the monthly mortgage revenue receipts of the pool

of assets and receives from the swap counterparty LIBOR plus 4 contractual spread on the same notional balance, the spread being sufficient ta caver the
intercompany loan and any expenses The Bank has a ‘back to back’ swap that 1s the mirror mage of the subsidiaries’ swaps

‘The swaps are valued based on an assumed amortisation profile of the pool of assets to the bond maturity date (assuming some annua! prepayment),

an assumed profile of customer receipts over this period, and LIBOR prediction usmg forward rates Swap cash flows are csscounted to present value using
mid-yleld curve zero coupon rates, ie no adjustment is made for credit losses, nor for transaction or any other costs

Investment properties

Investment properties within level 3 are valued by taking the original price, index linked to the balance sheet date using the relevant house price index
