41. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities contnued
Key considerations in the calculaton of fair values for loans and recewables and financial habsites at amor ised cost are as follows

Loans and advances to banks/deposits by banks

Loans and advances to banks include mterbank placements and items in the course of collection

The amortsed cost value of all loans and advances to banks are deemed to be a close approximation of ther far value due to ther short maturity The estimated
far value of fred mterest bearing deposits is based on discounted cash flows using prevailing money market interest rates for debts with similar credit nek and
remaming maturity

Loans and advances to customers

The fatr value of loans and advances to customers is calculated by segmentmg the overall balance mto Reta, Optimum and Corporate

1 Retail

Fixed rate loans and advances fo customers are revalued to fair value based on future interest cash flows (at funding rates) and principal cash flows discounted
using an appropnate market rate Forecast principal repayments are based on redemption at the earlier of maturtty of repricing date with some overiay for
historical behavioural experience where relevant The eventual timmg of future cash flows may be cifferent from the forecast due to unpredictable customer
behaviour it is assumed there 1s no far value adjustment required m respect of nterest rate movement on standard vanable rate assets

a Optimum

Fair values have been calculated using an origination spread income approach Under thts approach, value ts measured by determining discounted expected
cash flows, derived using redemption profiles from the portfolio and applying an origination spread which reflects the difference between current market rates
for products with similar characteristics and risk protiles and the actual rates the portfolio is generating

iw Corporate

As part of the imptementation of the Bank's strategy for Non-core assets, certain assets have either already been sold after the year end or plans to sell are well
advanced For these assets, the fair value can therefore be determined from the actual sale price achieved or expected to be received

For other corporate assets an expected cashflow income approach has been used Under this approach, value ts measured by determming expected cash flows
derived using redemption profites from the portfolio and then considering credit costs, funding costs and tax to derive cash flows which are discounted at an
appropriate blended cost of capital

The fair vatue of loans and advances to customers 1s 92% of the carrying value as at 31 December 2013 (2012 when applying 2013 methodology 90%)
The overall fair values are less than par primarily due to two main factors for Non-core loans in particular

1 Customer interest rates are below the market rate at the balance sheei date unt! expected maturity or the repricing date, If earlier, and
2 Credit risk adjustments due fo incurred and expected future credit losses

Investment securities

Fair value ts based on available market prices Where this information is not available, far value has been estimated using quoted market prices for secunties
with similar credit, maturity and yield characteristics

Customer accounts

The estimated far value of deposits with no stated matunty, which imcludes non-interest bearing deposits, is the arnount repayable on dernand The estimated
fay value of fixed interest bearing deposits and other borrowmgs without quoted market prices is based on future interest cash flows (at funding rates) and
principal cash flows, discounted using an appropnate market rate

At31 December 2012, the methodology used to estate the fair value of loans and advances to customers is summarised in note 39 of the financial
statements of the 2012 Annual Report and Accounts The key aspects of this methodology, with reference to the financial mstrument nsks that have the
greatest impact on fair value, are summarised below

a) Expected tives of financial instruments were based on updated forecasts of prepayment curves, based on recent actual experience in each key portfolio

b) Interest rate adjustments were catculated on the non-admunistered rate (fixed and LIBOR/base tracker rates) across the Retail, Optmum and Corporate
books The cash flows on these books were discounted using LIBOR/SONIA yield curves The mterest rate adjustment calculated at 31 December 2012
was £961m (positive), from which the far value adjustment for hedged risk (£354m) was removed as this was already included n the carrymg value of the
loans and advances, leaving a total nterest rate nsk adjustment of £607m (positive)

Credit risk adjustments were made based on the expected loss calculations for ail the portfolios reported in the FSAQ04 regulatory retums The total credit
nisk adjustment applied was £1,137m (negative), from which the accounting provisions held at 31 December 2012 (£567m) were deducted to ieave a total
net adjustment of £570m The credit risk adjustment was also applied to ex-Britannia loans and advances, which were acquired at fair value m 2009, as the
credit risk component of the valuation at that time proved to be too small by 31 December 2012 The credit nsk adjustment was considered to represent a
reasonable proxy for lifetrne expected losses plus an unquantified risk premium adjustment

d) The percentage difference between the far value and the carrymg value of total loans and advances was compared with similar financial inshtutions and was
broadly consistent

Securitisation vehicles

The beneficial ownership of the loans and advances to customers sold to securitisation vehicles by the subsidranes of the Bank fail the derecognition cntera, and
consequently, these foans remain on the balance sheets of the sellers Each seller therefore recognises a deemed loan financial hability on its balance sheet and
an equivalent deemed loan asset ts held on each securitisation company's balance sheet The deemed loans are repaid as and when principal repayments are
made by customers against these transferred loans and advances

The securitisation vehicles have issued fixed and floating rate notes which are secured on the loans and advances to customers The notes are redeemable in
part from time to time, such redempttons being limited to the net capital received from mortgagors in respect of the underlying assets

The Bank retains substantially all of the nsks and rewards of ownership The Bank benefits to the extent to which surplus income generated by the transferred
mortgage portfolios exceeds the admunstration costs of those mortgages The Bank continues to bear the credit risk of these mortgage assets

42. Post balance sheet events

tis a requirement of IAS 10 (Events after the balance sheet date) that these financial statements reflect events arising after the 31 December 2013
The following events have occurred between 31 December 2013 and 10 Aprit 2014 (the date of approval of these financial statements) and represent
‘non adjusting’ post balance sheet events

Employees

Dung the year, the mayonty of the Bank’s employees had contacts with CFS Management services Linuted (CFSMS) and the cost of their services was
recharged to subsidiaries, including the Bank Foliowing the legal separation of the Bank from the wholly owned subsidiary of The Co-operative Group,
employment contracts for the majority of these employees transferred from CFSMS to the Bank The transfer took place under the Transfer of Lindertakings
(Protection of Employment) Regulations 2006 Further transfers will occur m 2014 as the Bank completes sts separation

Unity Trust Bank pic

The Bank owns a 26 7% shareholding in Unity Trust Bank wia a subsidiary undertaking Unity Trust Bank 1s considered to be a subsidiary undertaking of the Bank
as the Bank eects a majority of the Directors and appoints the Charman and Managing Director This provides the power to contro)

On 14 January 2044, the Bank announced that it was in discussions wath the Board of Unity Trust Bank about a potential sale of ts 26 7% shareholding
Discussions are at an early stage and any decision on a changed ownership structure would be subject to regulatory approval

Britannia International Limited

On 10 January 2014, The Co-operative Bank ple announced that in ine with its wider strategy of simplifying the business it 1s to gradually wind down Britannia
International operations over the course of 2014

1. Restatement

Historically the intangible asset for the core IT banking system replacement had been recorded in the books of CFS Management Services Ltd (CFSMS),

{a service company which was a fellow Co-operative Banking Group subsidiary), with appropriate recharge to the Company via intercompany balances payable
The development of the core {T banking system was funded by the Bank CFSMS had tmited other assets with which to absorb any potential losses that may
have arisen due to difficulties in development

The Directors have revewed and reconsidered the accounting treatment of the intangible asset in development and all other assets held on the balance sheet

of CFSMS which were used solely by the Company The Directors have concluded that the Company was substantially exposed to the risks and rewards of these
assets and after considering the funding of the asset and CFSMS's lack of assets to absorb losses, the appropriate accounting treatment would be to hold these
assets on the balance sheet of the Company This has been reftected m the Bank and Company Annual Report and Accounts

The effect of the prior year restatement on the Company ts.

7. Derivative financial instruments

The Company has entered, as prncipal, into various derivatives either as a trading actly, which includes propnetary transactons and customer facilitation, or
as a hedging actnty for the management of interest rate nsk, equity risk and foreign exchange rate nsk Positive and negative fair values have not been netted
as the Company does not have a legal right of offset

Derivatives held for trading purposes

The trading transactions are wholly terest rate related contracts including swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures
Trading transactions include derivatives where the Company enters into a transaction to accommodate a customer together with the corresponding hedge
transaction The Company no longer holds denvatives for tracing purposes

Non-trading derivatives

Non-trading transactions comprise derivatives held for hedging purposes to manage the asset and habihty positions of the Company Denvatives used to manage
interest rate related positions mctude swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures The foreign exchange rate positions are
managed using forward currency transactions and swaps Equity nsk is managed using equity swaps

During the year the Company has entered into fair value hedges to mitigate price movements due to interest rate sensitivities

Indemnification

The Bank has an indemnification agreement, accounted for as a guarantee under IFRS 4, with CFSMS m which the Bank has agreed to indemnify CFSMS
against all and any kabikty, Joss, damage, costs and expense ansing from the agreement (under which CFSMS provides certan assets and services to Bank)
This agreement will remain mn place until a new agreement is put in place following separation as part of the LME

Conduct issues

Given the high level of scrutmy regarding financial nstitutions' treatment of customers and business conduct from regulatory bodies, the media and politicians,
there ts a nk that certain aspects of the Bank's current or historic business, including, amongst other things, mortgages and relationship banking, may be
determined by the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) and other regulatory bodies or the courts as not being conducted in accordance with applicable laws

or regulations, or far and reasonable treatment in their opinion

In particular, there 1s currently a significant regulatory focus on the sale practices and reward structures that financial institutions have used when seling

financial products. There ts a risk that there may be other regulatory investigations and action against the Company in relation to conduct and other issues that '
the Bank is not presently aware of, including investigations and actions against it resulting from alleged mis-sellng of financial products or the ongoing servicing

of those financial products The nature of any future disputes and legal, regulatory or ather investigations or proceedings into such matters cannot be predicted

in advance Furthermore, the outcome of any ongomng disputes and legal, regulatory or other investigattons or proceedings ss difficult to predict

Consumer Credit Act issues

The Consumer Credit Act regulates consumer lending and governs the way in which entities, including banks, providing consumer credit to retail customers

carry out business From 4 April 2014 the Financial Services and Markets Act 2000 also applies alongside certamn retained provisions of the Consumer Crecit

Act The Consumer Credit Act includes very detailed, prescriptive and highly technicat requirements for lenders affecting customer documentation and which, '
in turn, impact how operational processes and IT systems are configured While the Bank has identified certain instances where its documentation or processes

have not been fully compliant with the technical requirements and has provided accordingly, there may be other instances which have not yet been identified

Breaches may have the effect of tnggering periods of non-compliance during which an affected customer ts not liabie to pay interest

Interest refunds would therefore need to be made in certain cases where a period of non-complance has been previously triggered The Bank ts considering the
operational implementation of the refunding of interest to customers where applicable The Bank has not yet determined all aspects of such processes The Bank
has made no provisions to pay interest on the amount of any refund

An amount of £109 5m (2012 £nif) has been recognised to refund interest following rdentification of a number of breaches of the requements of the
Consumer Credit Act As part of this process of identification, detailed and technical legal analysis has been carried out as to whether breaches of the technical
requirements have in fact occurred, the nature of those breaches and the consequences which follow (which differ according to the precise nature of the
breach) Such legal analysis by its nature involves judgement and assessment of the facts of particular circumstances In the event that such legal analysis

and judgements are determined to be wrong, the Company could be exposed to a material additional fabilty The amount of £109 5m whuch has been provided
Is the best estimate of the liability based on legal analysis

These mclude

The Sir Christopher Kelly review was announced on 12 July 2013 Sw Chnstapher was jontly appomted by the Co-operative Group Limited and the Bank
to review the events that led to the Bank’s capital action plan to address its £1 5bn capital shortfall It ss looking at the decision to merge the Bank with the
Britannia Building Society m 2009 and the proposed acquisition of the Verde assets of Lloyds Banking Group It will include an analysis of strategic decision
making, management structures, culture, governance and accounting practices and aspects of the role of the Bank's Auditors The intention ts for the findings
to be publicised at The Co-operative Group's Annual General Meeting m May 2014

© The Treasury announced by press release on 22 November 2013 that it would conduct an independent investigation into events at The Co-operative
Bank ple and the circumstances surrounding them from 2008 mclucing the Verde transaction and Britannia merger The investigation will nclude a review
of the conduct of regulators and Government but is not anticipated to commence unt it is clear that it will not prejudice the outcome of the FCA and PRA
enforcement mvestigations

Mortgage securitisation representations and warranties

Pensions

There ts uncertamty over the amount that the Bank will have to pay while it conunues to participate nm Pace The Bank's obligations to contribute to Pace would
increase significantly # another large employer in Pace becomes insolvent while the Bank continues to participate {f the Bank seeks to address these risks by
terminating its participation, the default position ts that matenal abilities in respect of the deficit in Pace will arse The Co-operative Group and the Bank have
agreed to enter into good faith discussions to manage this by reaching agreement so that the liabihties properly attributable to the Bank (and an equivalent
proportion of assets) would be transferred to a separate scheme, or a segregated section of Pace, on the Bank's exit but, as no arrangements have yet been
agreed (and there ts a risk that none can be agreed), there is uncertainty over the amount that the Bank will have to pay in the event that it exits Pace

The Pace scheme ts not sectionalised and operates on a ‘last man standing’ basis In the event that other participating employers become insolvent and the
full statutory debt is not recovered on msolvency, the Bank would become Jiable for the remaining tiabtlittes

Other pensions risks and uncertamties include the risk to the Bank’s capital and funds from the Bank's exposure to scheme labilities (to the extent labiltes
are not met by scheme assets), msks inherent in the valuation of scheme jiabilittes and assets, risks regarding the split of habilities between the Bank and other
participating employers while the Bank continues to participate in Pace and on exit from Pace and, in respect of the Bntannia Scheme, risks on separation from
The Co-operative Group

It's not practicable to provide an estimate of the financial impact of these matters or what effect if any that these matters may have upon the Bank's operative
revenues, Cash flows or financial position in any period

Former Britannia Building Society pension scheme guarantee

Following the transfer of engagements of Bntannia Bulldng Society, the Britannia pension scheme transferred to CFSMS Under the terms of this transfer the
Company entered into a deed of guarantee to provide assurance io the trustees of the pension scheme to support CFSMS in meeting Its funding obligations to
the scheme should CFSMS be unable to pay its obligations as they fall due However, The Co-operative Group has undertaken to procure that if CFSMS ts not
controlled by the Bank, GFSMS wil not take or omit to take any action without the Bank's prior agreement if the result would be to mcrease the Bank's habilities
of contributions in respect of the Britannia Scheme The most recent actuanal report indicated that the funding deficit n the Britannia Scheme as at Apri 2013
was £61 Om

The Bank will need to manage the habilties that could arise on separation from The Co-operative Group, ds will require the co-operation of the Trustees of the
Brtanma Scheme, which may not be forthcoming

Tax treatment of separation

Until separation of the Bank from The Co-operative Group is complete, and the new arrangements referred to m the CFSMS-Bank Framework Agreement
as detailed in note 39 to the Bank's financial statements have been entered into, the Bank will continue to be responsible for indemnifynng CFSMS under
the CFSMS-Bank Services Agreement {t's anticipated that the Bank wall become the owner of the assets currently held by CFSMS for the provision of
services exclusively to the Bank ~ it expected that this will be achieved in a VAT efficient manner, but sf this cannot be done, there 1s a potential VAT
cost of approximately £26m on the transfer of the Bank exclusive assets to the Bank.

23. Contingent liabilities and commitments continued

As detatted mn note 3 to the Bank financial statements, the Directors have renewed and reconsidered the accounting treatment of the intangible asset in
development and all other assets held on the balance sheet of CFSMS which were used solely by the Bank. The Dyectors have concluded that the Bank was
substantally exposed to the neks and rewards of these assets and after consklenng the funding of the asset and CFSMS's lack of assets to absorb losses, the
appropriate accounting treatment would be to hold these assets on the balance sheet of the Bank The Bank has applied an approach to the tax and accounting
treatment of the Bank exchisive assets However, if, and that to the extent that, there is a change to this treatment, there may be an additonal tax charge

There wil contwiye to be VAT charges incurred m respect of any assets that are supphed to the Bank under the CFSMS-Bank Services Agreement or the
CFSMS-Bank Framework Agreement (as apphcable) As such, the Bank will look to mitigate these VAT charges through the transfer of all or some of such
assets to the Bank as soon as possible

27. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities continued

The carrying values of financial instruments measured at fair value are determined m compliance with the accounting policies (in note 1 to the Bank Financial
Statements} and according to the following hierarchy

Level 1 - Quoted market prices in active markets

Financial mstruments with quoted prices for identical mstruments in active markets The best evidence of fair value ts a quoted market price in an actively
traded market

Level 2 - Valuation techniques using observable inputs

Financial instruments with quoted prices for similar mstruments in active markets or quoted prices for identical or similar instruments in Inactive markets and
financial mstruments valued using models where all significant inputs are observable

The valuation techniques used to value these instruments employ only observable market data and relate to the following assets and habilines

Loans and advances to customers

Loans and advances to customers include corporate foans of £125 5m (2012 £142 4m) which are fair vatued through come or expense using observable
inputs. Loans held at fair value are valued at the sum of all future expected cash flows, discounted using a yield curve based on observable market inputs
Investment securities ~ available for sale

Farr value is based on available market prices Where this information is not avaitable, fair value has been estimated usmg quoted market prices for securities
with similar credit, maturity and yield characteristics

Derivative financial instruments

Over-the-counter (ie non-exchange traded) derivatives are valued using valuation models which are based on observable market data Valuation models calculate
the present value of expected future cash flows, based upon ‘no arbitrage’ principles The Bank enters into vanilla foreign exchange and interest rate swap
derivatives, for which modelling techniques are standard across the industry Examples of nputs that are generally observable mclude foreign exchange spot

and forward rates, and benchmark interest rate curves

Investment properties
Investment properties are carned at far value Fair value 1s calculated by using recent valuations of ndividual assets within the portfoko, mdex inked to the
balance sheet date using the relevant house price index where appropriate

Customer accounts - capital bonds

The estimated fair value of customer accounts ~ capital bonds is based on independent third party valuations using forecast future movements in the
appropriate indices

Level 3 ~ Valuation techniques using unobservable inputs
This ts used for financial mstruments valued using models where one or more significant inputs are not observable
The small proportion of fmancial assets valued based on significant unobservable imputs are analysed as follows

Loans and advances to customers

Loans and advances to customers include 25 year fixed rate mortgages of £8 7m (2012 £11 2m) which are fay valued through sncome or expense using
unobservable inputs 25 year fixed rate mortgages are valued using future terest cash flows at the fixed customer rate and estimated schedule of customer
Tepayments Cash flows are discounted at a credit adjusted discount rate, the credit adjustment is based on the average margin of new long dated (five years
or greater) fixed rate busmess wntten m the fast six months, and subject fo quarterly rewew The eventual ting of future cash flows may be different from that
forecast due to unpredictable customer behaviour, particularly on a 25 year product The valuation methodology takes account of credit risk and has increased
the vatuation by £0 8m mn 2013 (2012 £0 5m increase) A reasonable change m the assumptions would not result in any maternal change in the valuatton

27. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities continued

Equity shares

Equity shares primanty relate to mvestments held in Vocalink Lumited which are unquoted shares The valuation of these shares is based on the Company's
percentage shareholding and the net asset value of the Company according to ils most recently published financial statements

Amounts owed to Co-operative Bank undertakings

Amounts owed to Co-operative Bank undertakings are to the Sik Road Finance Number One plc (Silk Rd 1) subsidiary, relating to the legal transfer of loans and
advarices on securttisation The amounts are fair valued to eliminate an accounting raismatch of ihe swap derivative discussed below

Revaluation of the £1,466 4m (2012 £1,764 4m) mortgage pool from carrying to faur value is based on assumed timing of future mortgage capital and revenue
receipts, discounted to present value using a credit adjusted discount rate

The amortisation profile 1s based on a redemption profile, assuring some annual prepayment, which is extended to the earlier of the mortgage matunty date
or the step-up date, the earliest contractual maturity of the debt securities 1 issue when the balance outstanding on the notes may be repaid, this falls on

24 March 2015 Similarly, the revenue receipts are calculated based on the redemption profite, but extended until the earler of the mortgage maturity date or
the step-up date For fixed rate mortgages, revenue receipts are based on fixed customer rates within the assumed amortisation profile For tracker, SVR and
discount products, revenue receipts are assumed to be based on forward Bank of England base rates plus the product margins Fixed and tracker mortgages
are assumed to revert to SVR at the end of any offer period Alt mortgages in the mortgage pool were originated pre 31 December 2007

Derivative financial instruments
Denvative financial instruments in the form of interest rate swaps have been entered into between the Bank and its subsidiaries, and external counterparties

The purpose of the swaps ts to convert the fixed and base rate Inked revenue receipts of the pool of mortgage assets to the same LIBOR linked basis as the
intercompany loan Under this swap arrangement the Bank's subsidiaries pay to the swap counterparty, the monthly mortgage revenue receipts of the pool
of assets and receives from the swap counterparty LIBOR plus a contractual spread on the same notional balance, the spread being sufficient to cover the
intercompany loan and any expenses The Bank has a ‘back to back’ swap that is the mirror image of the subsidiaries’ swaps

The swaps are valued based on an assumed amorttsation profile of the poo! of assets to the bond maturity date {assuming some annual prepayment), an
assumed profile of customer receipts over this pertod, and LIBOR prediction using forward rates Swap cash flows are discounted to present value using
mid-yeld curve zero coupon rates, ie no adjustment is made for credit losses, nor for transaction or any other costs

In addition dervative liabiities clude a credit default swap The credit default swap balance has arisen, as on 28 January 2013, the Company entered into a
transaction to transfer a mezzanine portion of the nsk in a portfolio of residential mortgage loans to third party investors, via a special purpose vehicle, Calico
Finance Number One Limited The valuation takes on market MBS spreads, tess the onginal traded spread multiplied by the notional, and the duration, then
discounted These spreads include the probability of defautt

Investment properties

Investment properties are carned at fair value Fair value 1s calculated by using recent valuations of individual assets within the portfolio, index inked to the
balance sheet date using the relevant regional house price index, where appropriate

Key considerations in the calculation of fair values for loans and receivables and financial labilibes at amortised cost are as follows

Loans and advances to banks/deposits by banks

Loans and advances to banks include interbank placements and tems in the course of collection

The amortised cost value of all loans and advances to banks are deemed to be a close approximation of ther far value due to them short maturity The estimated
far value of fixed interest beanng deposits ss based on discounted cash flows using prevailing money market interest rates for debts with sunilar credit risk and
remaming maturity

Loans and advances to customers
The fam value of loans and advances to customers 1s calculated by segmenting the overall balance into Retail and Corporate

1 Retail

Fixed rate loans and advances to customers are revalued to fair value based on future interest cash flows (at funding rates) and pnncipal cash flows discounted
using an appropriate market rate Forecast principal repayments are based on redemption at the earler of matunity or repricing date with some overlay for
historical behavioural experience where relevant The eventual timmg of future cash flows may be different from the forecast due to unpredictable customer
behaviour It's assumed there 1s no fair value adjustment required in respect of mterest rate movement on standard vanable rate assets

u Corporate
As part of the implementation of the Company's ext strategy, certain assets have either already been sokd durmg the year or plans to sell are well advanced
For these assets, the fair value can therefore be determmned from the actual sale price achieved or expected to be received

For other Corporate assets an expected cashflow derived income approach using redemption profile has been used Under this approach, value ts measured by
determing expected cash flows from the portfolio and then considermg crecht casts, funding costs and tax to derive equity cash flows which are discounted at
an appropnate blended cost of capital

27. Fair values of financial assets and liabilities contnued

Investment secunties

Fair value is based on available market prices Where this information is not available, far value has been estimated using quoted market prices for securities
with similar credit, maturity and yield characteristics

Amounts owed by Co-operative Bank undertakings

Amounts owed by Co-operative Bank undertakings include debt securities and subordinated debt held in the Bank’s own securitisation vehicles Fair value
is based on available market prices, where these are not available quoted market prices for similar debt securities, with adjustments to take into account the
subordination of the particular foan note, have been used to estimate far value

Customer accounts

The estimated far value of deposits wth no stated matunty, which incluces non-mnterest bearing deposits, is the amount repayable on demand The estimated
fair value of fixed interest bearing deposits and other borrowmngs without quoted market prices is based on future interest cash flows (at funding rates) and
principal cash flows, discounted usmg an appropnate market rate

Debt securities in issue and other borrowed funds

The aggregate fair values are calculated based on quoted market prices For those notes where quoted market prices are not available, fair value has been
estimated using quoted market prices for securttes with similar credit, maturity and yield characteristics

The fair value of debt securities in issue Is significantly above the carrying value as 2 result of the carrying value being net of merger fair value adjustments
The carrying values of debt securities in issue are expected to crease, as the merger fair value adjustments continue to unwind, by £110m m 2014, £150m
in 2015, £180m in 2016 and by £60m in 2017

2012 comparatives (as reported)

The above table provides the 2012 fair value comparatives as reported in the 2012 Annual Report and Accounts and the 2012 far value camparatives
Te-presented using the 2013 methodology {as described above) The most significant difference mn fair value ansing from the two methodologies is on
Loans and advances to customers The following section summarises the methodology adopted for the 2012 Annual Report and Accounts

At 31 December 2012, the methodology used to estimate the fair value of loans and advances to customers Is summarised in note 39 of the financial
statements of the 2012 Annual Report and Accounts The key aspects of thts methodology, with reference to the fiancial instrument risks that have the
greatest impact on fair value, are summarised below

a) Expected lives of financial instruments were based on updated forecasts of prepayment curves, based on recent actual expenence in each key portfolio

b) Interest rate adjustments were calculated on the non-administered rate (fixed and LIBOR/base tracker rates) books across the Retail, Optimum
and Corporate books The cash flows on these books were discounted using LIBOR/SONIA yield curves The mterest rate adjustment calculated at
31 December 2012 was £961m (positive), from which the fair value adjustment tor hedged risk (£354m) was removed as this was already included in
the carrying value of the loans and advances, leaving a total interest rate risk adjustment of £607m (positive)

¢) Credit risk adjustments were made based on the expected loss calculations for all the portfolios reported in the FSAQ04 regulatory returns The total credit
nsk adjustment applied was £1,137m (negative), from which the accounting provisions held at 31 December 2012 (£567m) were deducted to leave a total
net adjustment of £570m The credit risk adjustment was also applied to ex-Britannia loans and advances, which were acquired at far value n 2009, as the
credit risk component of the valuation at that time proved to be too small by 31 December 2012 The credit risk adjustment was considered to represent a
reasonable proxy for Itetime expected losses plus an unquantified risk premium adjustment

d) The percentage difference between the fair value and the carrying value of total foans and advances was compared with similar financial insttutions and
was broadly consistent There were no significant observations ansing from this revew
Amounts owed to Co-operative Bank undertakings

Amounts owed to other Co-operative Bank undertakings retate to deemed loans owed to the Bank's own securitisation vehicles, these have been modelled
using expected cash flows, based on redemption profiles, discounted al a market rate

Until the second half of 2013, The Co-operative Banking Group mamtained common governance, organisational and risk management stuctures, which had
been developed and implemented across its subsidiaries including the Bank

tn 2013 the Bank put m place its own revised, updated and independent Risk Management Framework and policies, which have been adopted by the Bank
Executive and the Board The Bank's new risk management framework reflects the previous system in many respects, but it continues to be refined to meet
the Bank's specific requirements

As a result of the new Risk Management Framework, the Bank 's now solely responsible for determining its principal nsks, and the level of acceptable nsks,
as stipulated m the Bank's risk appetite statement, thus ensuring that there is an adequate system of risk management so that the levels of capital and liquidity
held are consistent with the risk profile of the business

Forbearance

The Bank, for reasons relating to the actual or apparent financial stress of a borrower, grants a concession whether
temporarily or permanently to that borrower A concession may involve restructuring the contractual terms of a
debt (such as an extension of the maturity date or any weakening of the secunty structure or adjustment/non
enforcement of covenants) or payment in some form other than cash, such as an equity interest in the borrower

Foreclosure

The legal process by which a lender, eg the Bank, obtams a court order in order to terminate a borrower's equitable
fight of redemption This legal action 1s required when a borrower has matenally failed to comply with the agreed
terms and condittons of the loan, eg by defaulting (see Forbearance)

Forward rate agreement

A legal contract, governing the terms and conditions of a forward or future, m which the buyer pays a fixed rate
of interest to the seller in return for the seller's floating rate (see Forwards, Futures and Swaps)

Forwards

Non-standardised contracts, traded over the counter, between two parties to buy or sell financial mstrumenis at
a specified future time at a price agreed today {see Over The Counter)

* ‘Standardised’ businesses lack sufficient mformation upon which detailed credit analysis can be undertaken
for which models have yet to be developed,

© ‘Strong’ businesses have little competition, high demand, significant governmental support anc
enforceable collateral,

© ‘Good busmesses have limited competition, stable demand, good govemmental support and
enforceable collateral,

© ‘Satisfactory’ businesses have a broad competition base with limited tevels of demand, governmental support

* ‘Weak’ businesses operate at a disadvantage to compettion, display a declining trend of demand, with no

© Default’ businesses are considered unlikely to repay thei credit obligations (see Impaired loans)
